A bad name is refused and the connection closes. 3. `identify` with `{"member": "w-1", "token": "..."}`: the credential token, checked by `Gateway::verify_credential` (the check `klatch serve --stdio` makes for a key bundle: token, expiry, revocation, a refusal recorded without the token). A live credential gives a **Verified** principal, and every later request is checked live again like any verified principal's. A bad token, another member's name with the token, a name given together with a token, or a token without a member each end the connection with an error: nothing falls back to a weaker name. The bundle file itself (which also holds the store key) is never sent; only the member and the token are. The `hello` frame goes first, at once, with the case 1 principal, so a client that waits for it never waits for anything else. `identify` is recognised only as the first request; the daemon answers it with the member and attribution, then the session starts without a second `hello`. After any other first line, `identify` is an ordinary unknown method. This frame is the one thing the daemon adds to the protocol (PROTOCOL.md section 9); a session of `klatch serve --stdio` has no such method. Choosing `identify` over a preamble line before `hello` was settled by reading the Rust client, which waits for `hello` before it sends anything (a preamble the server waits for would deadlock it).

   **The socket file.** The directory must exist, belong to the daemon's uid and not be writable by group or others (a named refusal: "not safe"); the path must fit `sun_path` (a refusal naming the limit in bytes: 103 on macOS, 107 on Linux; see #778); the file is created with mode 0600 (umask 0177 for the bind, then set again); a file that is not a socket is never removed; a socket nobody listens on (a connect probe is refused) is removed; a LIVE daemon (the probe connects) makes startup fail ("already listening"), the socket is never stolen; the file is removed on a clean exit, and only if it is still the file this daemon made (device and inode). A directory owned by another uid is refused by the same function; it is unit-tested, not tested with a real second user.

   **Limits.** A frame is at most `MAX_LINE_BYTES` (1 MiB): the first line over it gets a `frame.too_large` refusal and the connection closes; a later one meets the session's own refusal and is skipped to its newline, never stored. A connection that sends no first frame within `--first-frame-secs` (default 10) is closed; one that has spoken is never closed for silence (a subscriber waits for pushes; a dead peer shows at the next heartbeat write). At most `--max-connections` (default 32) at once: the excess gets one refusal frame (`daemon.connections`) and is closed. Output goes through the session's bounded queue and stall limit (#147), so a consumer that stops reading is closed.

   **Stopping.** SIGTERM and SIGINT stop accepting, close every open connection (its session ends), join every thread, drop the gateway (the owner thread closes the store), remove the socket and exit 0. Nothing runs after the process ends.

   **Network-free.** The only listener is a Unix socket. The `klatch` binary gained nothing (its `no_telemetry` test is untouched). `klatch-server`'s scan test was changed deliberately: `UnixListener`, `UnixStream`, `getsockopt`, `getpeereid`, `SO_PEERCRED` and `LOCAL_PEERPID` are allowed in `src/daemon.rs` and nowhere else; the module has exactly one `UnixListener::bind` and one `UnixStream::connect` (the probe of its own path); TCP types stay forbidden in it; the uid check is asserted to come before the connection thread; outbound connects, name resolution, HTTP clients and `Command` stay forbidden everywhere.

   **Tests** (`klatch-server/tests/daemon.rs`, the daemon as a child process against a temp store and a short socket directory under /tmp; `src/daemon.rs` unit tests; `gateway/shared_session_tests.rs`). Written first for the daemon (all 18 were red because the subcommand did not exist); the `Backend` refactor and its library tests were written code first, guarded by the existing session tests. Covered: two concurrent connections post through one gateway with dense sequence numbers 1 to 20 and a third reads them in order as a stream; each identity tier; mode 0600; SIGTERM and SIGINT remove the socket; unsafe directory modes, a missing directory and a regular file in the way; the too-long path; a stale socket and a live daemon; the frame cap (first line and later line); the connection cap and a freed slot; the first-frame timeout; garbage on one connection with another unaffected; a store that cannot be opened. **Conformance run:** one scripted scenario (join, three posts, a handoff post, history, subscribe from 0, ack, read, claim, an unknown method) for two members in turn, through the session loop in the test process (what `klatch serve --stdio` runs over a `Gateway`) and through the daemon: every frame after the identity, with times and message ids removed, is equal, and the sequence numbers are 1 to 4 then 5 to 8 in both. What that does not cover: the consumer libraries' own conformance suites (`clients/`, `proto/klatch/tests/client.rs`) cannot run over the socket until the client libraries can connect to it (#1095); a connection from another uid and a socket directory owned by another uid cannot be made here (unit tests with a fake credential); Linux `SO_PEERCRED` was written and compiled on macOS only.

   **Not done.** The client libraries' socket connect (#1095, the Rust client was not a small change: it holds a child process). Sealed labels and the harness requirement per connection (#1096: the shared gateway loads no per-member label keys, and a connection joins as the generic floor). A bench scenario: the bench cannot drive a `klatch-server` child yet (#1078). No TCP, no TLS, no proxy identity: those are the later steps of epic #13 (#109, #110, #108).

112. **SSH transport: identity from a forced command (#107, epic #13; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** ssh carries the bytes into `klatch serve --stdio`; klatch opens no socket and links no ssh code (the `no_telemetry` test is unchanged). The administrator writes `command="klatch serve --stdio --ssh-key LABEL",restrict ssh-ed25519 ...` per key in `authorized_keys`, so sshd runs the command line itself and the remote can only send bytes on standard input. The label names a key, never a member. Evidence is a new `PeerEvidence::SshForcedKey { label }` (kind `ssh-forced`); it never comes from a typed name, `KLATCH_NAME`, `KLATCH_DISCOVER`, `KLATCH_IDENTITY_FILE` or `SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND`, none of which `serve --ssh-key` reads, and `--as-member`, `--discover` or `--codex` beside it is an error.

   The mapping from label to member is the human's act at a terminal: `klatch admin ssh-key LABEL MEMBER` (and `--off`), a floor (`FLOOR_ACTIONS`), so no agent and not the admin agent can make or change it. It is stored in the policy table as `ssh-key:<label>` (no schema change), audited as `ssh-key` with `LABEL/MEMBER=on`. A label is 1 to 40 letters, digits, `.`, `_` or `-` (a bare word an `authorized_keys` line can carry); a reserved or invalid member name is `invalid`; a label already mapped to another member is refused (rule `ssh-key-mapped`, kept in the refusals) until it is unmapped, which names the member it is taken from. A member may have more than one label, which is how a key is rotated.

   `SshKeyIdentity` maps the evidence through the table: a mapped label is the member, `verified`; an unmapped label is unattributed (R23) and the identity fails closed on any other kind of evidence. `klatch serve --stdio --ssh-key LABEL` refuses to start for an unmapped label (rule `ssh-key-unmapped`, the next step `klatch admin ssh-key LABEL <member>`, a refusal row, no `hello`). A session started this way is fixed to its member (`Session::fixed_member`): a request with a `sender`, `from`, `as`, `as_member`, `caller`, `identity`, `principal` or `attribution` param, or a `member` param on a method that does not take a member as its target, is refused with rule `member-fixed` and not run.

   Verified by tests: `gateway/tests/ssh_key.rs` (the floor, audit, one member per label, bad names, identity), `session/tests/fixed_member.rs` and `session/fixed.rs`, `tests/ssh_forced_command.rs` (two keys, two members, run as child processes against a temporary store with a hostile environment: each posts only as itself, and a frame naming the other member or a forged identity field is refused), and the bench scenario `two_ssh_forced_command_keys_are_two_members_and_neither_can_claim_to_be_the_other`. The deployment guide is `clients/recipes/ssh-transport.md` (authorized_keys line, host key verification as mandatory, a `ControlMaster`/`ControlPersist` stanza with the latency effect stated as a hypothesis, threat notes).

   Known limits: the real sshd path was not run by the builders (no ssh or sshd is started in tests); klatch cannot see whether the client verified the host key (the guide makes it mandatory); a `member-fixed` refusal is not kept in the refusals and not counted by the breaker (#1084).

113. **Reaching a loopback-only klatch-server through a tunnel, and every HTTP route in the audit (#110, #161, epics #13 and #152; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** Part of #110 is built without a new listener or a trust decision: the deployment guide `clients/recipes/behind-a-tunnel.md` (an ssh local forward to the loopback listener, a same-host mutual-TLS terminator, and the forced-command transport of #107 when a port is not needed) and the tests that pin the one rule every pattern relies on: identity is the worker credential and nothing a proxy adds. The listener stays loopback only and `bind_loopback` is untouched; no off-loopback bind is possible, and the refusal text still names #157 and #161.

   **Tests.** `forwarded_identity_headers_give_no_identity_and_change_none` (`klatch-server/tests/http_wire.rs`): every route is `401` with forwarded-address, forwarded-user and client-certificate headers and no credential (from a loopback source, as a same-host proxy would be), and a credential for `w-1` with headers naming `w-2` is a `w-1` session. `no_source_reads_a_forwarded_header_or_trusts_a_peer_address` (`klatch-server/tests/route_audit.rs`) scans every source file for those header names and `peer_addr`. `every_route_is_named_by_a_row_of_the_authorization_audit` finds each route constant and route arm of the crate and fails when no audit row names it; it was red first because the webhook intake route `/github` had no row (now `proto/audit/06a-klatch-server-webhook-intake/`). The guide has `clients/recipes/test_behind_a_tunnel_doc.py` (loopback-only commands, the identity and ignored-header statements, the same-host rule for a plaintext hop, `not run` for ssh and stunnel, links resolve).

   **Not built, by design.** Forwarded identity accepted from configured proxy addresses and audited when ignored (the rest of #110's done-when) would turn a header into an identity; it waits for a threat-model decision recorded on the ticket. Native TLS and client certificates (#108, #109, #157), the WebSocket and gRPC connectors (#112, #113) and remote exposure (#300, #1061) are blocked on the TLS library, certificate management and enrollment decisions, also on their tickets. The ssh and stunnel lines in the guide were not run.

114. **`klatch acp --stdio`: the read-only librarian offered to editors as an ACP agent (#115, epic #10; no schema or wire change; 2026-11-18).** DESIGN section 20 rule 13 names editors (ACP) as a later bridge and RESEARCH-NOTES section 15 names the librarian as the agent to offer. **Shape:** `session/acp.rs` is an adapter over `Session::call`, the same plain request path `klatch mcp --stdio` (#299) and `serve --stdio` use, so each query meets every check a wire request meets (guard, roles, labels, the receiver budget, the refusal record) and the adapter checks nothing of its own. **Identity:** the process's member, found as for every command; no prompt can name another caller (a test pins it). A member granted the librarian role is also held to `read-only` by the gateway; the command does not require the role, because the four looks it offers are open to any member under that member's own clearance and no write exists in it. **Queries:** the prompt is not given to a model. It is a fixed grammar on the first non-empty line: `read ROOM` (always a peek, so no cursor moves), `history ROOM [N]`, `who ROOM`, `claims ROOM` and `help`. Anything else, including `post`, `claim`, `release`, `dm`, an admin act or a second statement on the line, is answered with the help text and calls nothing, so text another member wrote can never become a call. A wire refusal or failure is returned as the answer text naming the rule and reason, never the matched content; messages come with a notice that they are data. **Protocol assumptions (written from memory of the public ACP specification, not re-read against it on 2026-11-18; ticket #869 to verify):** JSON-RPC 2.0, one message per line; `initialize` takes and returns an integer `protocolVersion` of 1 with `agentCapabilities` (`loadSession` false, no image, audio or embedded context), empty `authMethods` and `agentInfo`; `session/new` returns a `sessionId` and ignores `cwd` and `mcpServers`; `session/prompt` streams the answer as one `session/update` notification with `sessionUpdate` `agent_message_chunk` and a text content block, then responds with `stopReason` `end_turn`; `session/cancel` is a notification and is ignored, because a query is answered before the next line is read; `session/load` and `authenticate` are unsupported (`-32601`); an unknown `sessionId` is `-32602`. No permission requests, tool calls, file system or terminal requests to the client are made. **Not built:** a model-backed librarian that interprets free text (the grammar is the whole of it), `board` and other looks, streaming a long answer in several chunks, a network transport. **Tests (written after the code, so not red first; the module and the command were added together and the tests then run against them):** `src/session/acp_tests.rs` against a members' gateway with a granted librarian (initialize, sessions, read as peek, history, who, claims, a query above the caller's clearance refused for all four looks with nothing leaked, a cleared librarian reading, no write reachable from any prompt including smuggled second statements, an argument naming another caller with no effect, an ordinary member held to its own clearance, unknown methods, notifications and junk) and `tests/acp.rs` against the real binary (a query over stdio, looking moves no cursor and posts nothing, no prompt writes, a clean exit when input closes, `--stdio` required).

115. **Recipes for the launchers klatch itself ships, checked by a doc test (#126, #414; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** `clients/recipes/own-launchers.md` gives a recipe for a plain script launcher (the CI demo), a Makefile launcher, the team launcher, the integrator and the ask supervisor. **Why:** the orchestrator recipes of #414 need a person with desktop tools, but the launcher pattern is the same under all of them, so a reader gets checked recipes now. **What is checked** (`clients/recipes/test_own_launchers_doc.py`): each klatch command in the Makefile is one `launcher-demo.sh` runs in CI, `make -n` of every target runs nothing, every script and flag named is in that script's header, the third-party tools stay `not run`. **Limit:** the Makefile was not run against the real binary, and the page says so.

116. **The gateway is shared across threads by one owner thread and a channel (#140; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** **Question.** The daemon of #102 and every listener after it serve several connections at once, but `Gateway` and `Direct` are single-threaded. The two ways out were (A) make the gateway shareable (`Arc` and a lock, or a connection per thread) and (B) leave it alone and give it one owner thread that serves requests from connection threads over a channel.

   **What holds the single thread (read from the code).** - `Direct` holds an `Rc<Gateway<S>>` (`gateway/transport.rs`); so do the `member` module and the `admin` command. An `Rc` cannot cross threads at all. - `Gateway<S>` holds `Box<dyn Clock>`, `Box<dyn Guard>`, `Box<dyn Notifier>` and `Option<Box<dyn LeaseSource>>`, none with a `Send` bound. `Arc<Mutex<Gateway>>` needs `Gateway: Send`, so each of those traits (and each implementation, including the tests' `Rc<Cell<i64>>` clocks and the `SpyStore`'s `Cell` and `RefCell`) would need `Send`, and `SharedGateway` over a non-`Send` guard would stop compiling for callers. - `SqliteStore` holds one `rusqlite::Connection` (`Send`, not `Sync`) and a `RefCell<LabelKeys>` (`store/sqlite.rs`). The label keys are per principal and live in the store, so a store shared by callers with different keys is a different design, not a lock. - `Direct` also holds an optional `Box<dyn Harness>`, which has no `Send` bound. - The `Subscription` is plain data (no store handle); `poll` takes the gateway as an argument. `Transport` has no blocking call: a wait is the loop in `commands/wait.rs` of `wait_poll`, `change_token` and a sleep on the caller's thread.

   **Option A, an `Arc` and a lock.** Smallest at the call site, widest in the code: a `Send` bound on four traits and all their implementations, `Sync` for the store (so a `Mutex` around the connection, which is one writer anyway), and a decision on whether the guard, the clock read and the store write of a request run under one lock (they must, for the sequence and claim checks to mean what they do now). A connection per thread to the same SQLCipher file keeps SQLite's own write lock as the one writer but multiplies the label keys, the open cost (key read and schema check on each open) and the busy-retry behaviour (`Error::Busy`, not fair under load, `proto/CONCURRENCY.md`), and gives no common place to put the clock and the guard. Every request would also hold the lock for its whole length, so the same lock would serialize what the owner thread serializes in B.

   **Option B, an owner thread.** No change to `Gateway`, `Direct`, the store, the clock, the guard or the notifier: the gateway is built on the owner thread (the caller passes a closure that opens the store), so none of it has to be `Send`. One connection and one writer, as today. A request is one whole `Transport` call, so the order the guard, the clock and the store see is the order of the channel. The cost is one channel round trip and a copy of the arguments per call, against microseconds of store work (a read of 20 messages is 0.007 ms, `proto/CONCURRENCY.md`), and one thread that a slow request holds up for the others; the same is true of a lock in A.

   **Blocking calls.** Settled by the code, so nothing needed a decision: `wait` never reaches the gateway as a blocking call. The owner serves `wait_poll` and `change_token` (each a short request) and the caller sleeps between them on its own thread, so a bounded wait holds neither a lock nor the owner. The streaming subscription is a value the caller keeps and sends to the owner for the length of one `poll`.

   **Choice: B.** It is the smaller change (one new file, nothing edited in the engine), it keeps the one-writer rule by construction, and it does not touch the cross-process design of `proto/CONCURRENCY.md` (other processes still meet the same SQLite file through its lock). Shuttle cannot see SQLite, so a Shuttle test of this module would test the channel and not the store; it was not added (the real-thread tests below are the evidence).

   **What was built** (`gateway/shared.rs`). - `SharedGateway::spawn(build)` starts the owner thread, builds the gateway there with `build` (returning the error `build` failed with, or `Backend` if it panicked) and returns a handle that is `Clone`, `Send` and `Sync`. When the last handle is dropped the owner stops and joins, so the store connection is closed. - `SharedGateway::transport(principal)` gives a `SharedTransport` bound to one principal, like `Direct`. It implements all of `Transport` (the arguments are copied to the owner), with `with_harness` for the harness the edge learned (made on the owner thread for each request). - A request that panics fails that call with `Backend` and the owner keeps serving. - `change_token`: the store's token moves only when another connection commits, so a post made through the owner would not move it. The handle adds a count of posts made through handles, so an in-process wait wakes at once, and a commit by another process still moves the store's part. - The `klatch` binary is unchanged: it still builds its own `Direct`, and nothing in it uses this module.

   **Tests** (`gateway/shared_tests.rs`, a temp SQLCipher file, real threads): eight threads post 200 messages and the sequence is exactly 1 to 200, each message once and attributed to its own handle; four writers and a reader lose and duplicate nothing; eight threads claiming one path give one holder and seven `Conflict`s; a bounded wait returns within 1.5 s of another thread's post while a second caller is served at once during the wait; the owner stops when the last handle goes; a gateway that cannot be built is an error; a panicking request leaves the owner serving. The tests were written before the module (a compile failure was the red); the wait test's time bound was tightened after the first green run.

   **Not done.** The daemon itself (#102) and any listener; per-connection principals from peer evidence (the edge's job). A slow request holds up the others, as it would under a lock; a pool of readers on separate connections would be a later, measured change.

117. **Every way in is audited, and an issued credential is checked on every request (#161; no schema change).** `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` lists every wire method, every command and every `klatch admin` command with the check each meets and the test that proves it; `tests/authz_audit.rs` fails when one exists in the source and is not named there, and the zero-trust test now reads the whole gateway directory (it had missed eight files and 24 public methods, among them the role grants, `invite`, `request_crossing` and both review operations). The credential is no longer checked only when a process starts: `check_guard`, the one entry every guarded operation reaches, first refuses (rule `credential`) a `Verified` principal whose member holds a credential that is expired or revoked, reading the clock at each request; a store failure refuses, a principal with no credential record (proven another way) and an unattributed one are untouched, and the rule stays out of the breaker's count, so a stale session cannot stop a worker that was reissued. Not done, ticketed: the own-record methods skip the check (#835), the launcher commands rest on the same heuristic as `klatch admin` and issue credentials (#834), `release_crossings` takes no principal (#836), and who may issue stays the human's. The default lifetime (3600 seconds, at most 30 days) and renewal in place or by reissue are the human's to change; nothing here moves them.

118. **Issued credentials renew on use and have a hard cap (#161, epic #152; schema 31; 2026-11-19).** **Decision (the human, 2026-11-18).** An issued credential renews automatically on use, with no human step. The idle timeout is the credential's `ttl` (`klatch worker issue --ttl`, default 3600 seconds): each successful request pushes the expiry out to now plus the timeout. A hard cap from issue (`--max-life`, default 24 hours, at most 30 days, the existing `MAX_CREDENTIAL_TTL_SECS` ceiling) is never passed by renewal. Revoke still stops the credential at its next request, and each renewal is audited. **Tradeoff accepted:** a stolen credential that its thief keeps using lives until the cap or a revoke; a dormant one dies at the idle timeout.

       **Where.** `check_credential_live`, the first step of `check_guard`, already looks at the credential on every request of a `Verified` principal (item for #161, the authorization audit). When it finds the credential live it calls `renew_credential_on_use`, which reads the record and asks the store to renew: `CredentialStore::renew_credential(member, now, step)` moves `expires` to `min(now + idle, cap)` in one write transaction and returns the new expiry, or `None`. It renews nothing for a credential that is revoked, expired, unknown, or from before schema 31 (`cap` 0), and a refused request never renews, so an expired credential is not brought back by trying. A first expiry is the idle timeout or the cap, whichever is nearer.

       **Write and audit rate.** Renewing on every request would write and audit once per request. A renewal is written only when it gains at least `step` seconds, a tenth of the idle timeout clamped to 1 to 60 seconds, or when it reaches the cap. A burst of requests is therefore one write and one `renew-credential` audit row (`expires=N cap=M`, never the token); the expiry is at most `step` seconds shorter than "a full idle timeout from the last request". This is the one deviation from "each successful request pushes the expiry out", chosen so a busy worker does not fill the audit log; the idle timeout and the cap are exact.

       **Schema 31.** `credentials` gains `idle` and `cap` (both 0 by default). An existing credential keeps its own expiry and is never renewed, which is the behaviour it was issued under; reissue gives it the new rules. `NewCredential.max_life` 0 means no renewal (the store-level default); the gateway always passes the cap (24 hours when the launcher gives none).

       **Rotation.** Unchanged and explicit: revoke, then issue again (a reissue starts a new cap). There is no rotation in place and no refresh of the token itself; the token stays the same for the credential's life.

       **Not done.** The own-record methods skip `check_guard` and so neither check nor renew a credential (#835); `klatch worker list` still shows the current expiry, not the cap. **Tests (the renewal tests failed on assertions before the code):** `gateway/gateway/tests/credential_renewal.rs` (renewal by use, idle death, the cap second, the 24 hour default cap, revoke, audit once per burst, untouched principals, cap range, rotation), `store/sqlite/tests/credentials.rs` (first expiry, renewal and cap, the step, never renewed when revoked, expired, unknown or legacy), the schema 30 to 31 migration test, and `tests/worker.rs` (a real process kept alive past its idle timeout and stopped at its cap; `--max-life` limits).

119. **The member model: a stable id with the repository and directory klatch observed, and the default clearance of a new repository (#181, #191; schema 30; 2026-11-18).** **What exists.** A member is a harness session, and its name is the key of every table (the first name a session gets is kept, a second session with the same name gets a suffix from its session id, #170). That already meets "same-named sessions stay distinct" and "a rename keeps one member". **What this adds (R41, R42):** the table `member_attrs(member, member_id, repository, directory, ts)` (schema 30, an existing member has no row until it next acts), written by `ensure_member_attrs` each time a member runs a command: `member_id` is `m-` and the FNV-1a hash of the session id (the member name when there is no session), made once and kept for the member's life; `repository` is the `git:<name>-<hash>` id of the directory, empty outside a repository; `directory` is where the session started. Two sessions in one repository are two members that share a `repository` value, and `Store::members_in_repository` lists them. **The repository id is derived, never typed:** `git_common_dir` reads the files git lays out (a `.git` directory, or a `.git` file saying `gitdir:` whose `commondir` points back at the common directory) and runs no git process, so a worktree shares the id of the repository it was made from, and nothing in a directory can execute at that step. The scope a member works in uses the same derivation, so the two agree (a test). **Zero trust:** the attributes are observations by the host, not authority. A directory can hold a `.git` file naming another repository's git directory, so a member can make its repository value equal another's; therefore no check reads the attributes, and sharing a repository grants no clearance, room or access. Clearance and labels stay as they were (R8, R23). **Left out, and why:** the member name stays the key (the store keys, claims and messages are by name, and a change of key would touch every table; the id is a stable handle beside it, for display and for the repository grouping), and no wire method or command shows the attributes yet (a later change that does needs a row in `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md`). Delivery and claims still use the scope, which a caller may declare; moving them onto the derived repository is not done. **Default clearance of a new repository (#191, open question 39, decided by the human on 2026-11-18):** a repository's first session keeps the lowest clearance (public); nothing above public crosses until clearance is granted. Pinned by `a_first_seen_repository_session_holds_the_lowest_clearance_and_cannot_read_above_it`, which also shows that sharing a repository with a cleared member lifts nothing. No grant process is built; whether a judge in the lobby and in channels (the channel creator as the first judge) should grant clearance is a separate question ticket. **Tests:** `scope/attr_tests.rs` (a hand-made git layout with a worktree, absolute and relative gitdir, two sessions in one repository, a rename, a move, no directory), a real `git worktree` test, store tests for the table and its migration from schema 29. They were written before the code and failed to compile first (the functions did not exist), not on an assertion.

120. **The reference rules-based router is an ordinary member (#189, DESIGN 24.1 decision 7, #271; no schema or wire change; 2026-11-18). `klatch router [--room lobby] [--rule prefix=member]... [--once]` runs deterministic passes as the caller's own member. A pass reads the lobby's open asks, live claims and presence through the same guard-checked calls every member has (`join`, `open_asks`, `claims`, `who`), plans with `domain::plan_routes` (a pure function), creates the topic room by joining it and invites with `invite`. No model, no state beyond a set of invites it already made in this run.** **The rule.** The topic of an ask is its asker's first live claim by path order, other than the bookkeeping claims `ask:` and `pr:`. The room is `topic-<slug of the topic>` (so asks on one topic share a room). The members invited are present members (seen within the board window, not `done`, not parked) with a live claim that overlaps the topic by path component, or that an operator `--rule prefix=member` names, at most three, in name order; never the asker, the router, or a member that declined that ask as `cannot`. An ask a helper holds, an ask of the router itself, an ask whose asker holds no topic claim, and a topic that makes no room name are left alone: nothing is guessed from words.

   **Zero trust.** (1) It reads structure only: `AskFacts` carries an ask's number, sender, whether it is held and who declined it as `cannot`, and has no text field, so no wording of an ask can steer it (decision 8: messages are data). A claim path is the only text compared, as everywhere else. (2) It holds no capability a normal member lacks beyond create and invite, the guard-checked and audited capability of #188: under `room-creation granted` a router without `grant-rooms` is refused both and creates nothing; with it, the audit names the router for `create-room` and `invite`. It has no admin act, sets no label and no clearance, and posts only the content-free invite pointer. (3) Clearance: it cannot read clearances, so the gateway now refuses `invite` of a member below the room's label (rule `clearance`, recorded as a refusal, nothing posted). That check is for every inviter, not only the router. (4) A refused act is reported and not retried in the same run, so a refused router does not fill the refusal record or trip the breaker.

   **Limits, honestly.** Claims of two members in one scope cannot overlap (the claim itself refuses), so the evidence "holds a claim on the topic" arises between members with different scopes (repositories), with shared claims, or from an operator rule. It does not use the repository attribute of #181 (not on main when this was written); a router that invites by repository is a follow-up. It routes by claims, not by what an ask asks for, so an ask whose asker holds no claim is not routed (a model reading text is the later, propose-only mediator of #271). The set of invites made lives in memory: a restarted router invites again a member that is not yet in the room. It watches one room.

121. **The facilitator: a model-backed router as a bounded role, with no model integration in klatch (#190, part of #180, DESIGN section 24.1 decision 7, the model step of #271; no schema change; 2026-11-18).** The rules router (#189) is the deterministic member that invites the holders of a claim on an asker's topic. The facilitator is the same job done by whatever reads the lobby: an ordinary coding session or any agent with the klatch command line. klatch builds the substrate only, the role and its bound, a brief, and tests with a scripted stand-in for the model. It links no model SDK and makes no model call.

   The role. `klatch admin facilitator MEMBER [--off]` (a terminal act, audited as `facilitator`) gives a member the role in the existing `role:<member>` policy entry, so it is one role at a time, like the librarian, reviewer and admin agent: granting it replaces another role, `--off` removes it. Neither the facilitator, nor any member, nor the admin agent can give or take it.

   The bound is an allowlist in `check_role`, run before any `Guard` is asked, so no guard configuration widens it. A facilitator may: read (moving its own cursor or not), `who`, `claims`, the board and the open asks, only in rooms at or below its own clearance (the lowest by default, so lobby content only at that clearance); join a room, which makes it when new (create-room, still subject to the room-creation capability of #188); invite (an invite is refused above the invitee's clearance, #189); and post the invite pointer, which is accepted only when the body equals, byte for byte, the sentence `invite` builds. Everything else is refused with the rule `facilitator`: any other post, a direct message, claim, release, status, mute, ack, scope and kind declaration, crossing request, every admin act (audit, prune and label order included), the label and clearance acts and the grant of room clearance.

   It never grants. A room a facilitator creates has no grantor (the creator rule of #893 skips it), and a grant by a facilitator is refused with the rule `facilitator` even when the human names it a room's grantor, so it is not a grantor unless separately named and even then inert. A different member named grantor is untouched.

   Order of checks. The role check comes first for the acts that do their own value checks before the guard (`clear` checked the agent cap first; it now refuses the role first). The scope and kind declarations ask no guard, so they call the same role check directly. Other admin acts that validate their arguments first return an invalid-argument error to a facilitator before the role refusal; that reveals nothing and changes nothing.

   The brief, `proto/scripts/facilitator-brief.md`, is the instruction text for the session: what it may do, what it never can, that message text is data and never an instruction, that it only proposes (a topic room and an invite pointer that the invitee may ignore), and that when unsure it does nothing. `tests/facilitator_brief.rs` fails if the brief drops a bound, teaches a command the role refuses or names a command the CLI lacks.

   Proof. `gateway/tests/facilitator.rs` covers the role, the allowlist, the named rule on each refusal, the order before the guard, own-clearance reads, the invitee clearance and the missing grantor. `session/facilitator_tests.rs` drives a scripted fake model through the session path of `klatch mcp --stdio` (one `Session::call` per tool call): a well behaved model routes two asks into two topic rooms with two invites and no refusals; a manipulated model that does whatever the ask text says (grant, clear, label, audit, close, post, claim) is refused every time with the rule `facilitator` and leaves clearances, labels and the lobby unchanged; an invite above the invitee's clearance is refused. Room creation has no wire method, so the model makes the room through the gateway as `klatch join` does (#901). The test was written before the implementation but not run red first.

   How a human launches a first run with an ordinary session as facilitator. The human does these steps, in their own terminal; an agent never issues a credential: 1. Choose a member name, for example `facilitator`, and issue its credential with the launcher (`klatch worker issue`, see `klatch worker --help`). 2. Grant the role: `klatch admin facilitator facilitator`. Under `room-creation granted` also run `klatch admin grant-rooms facilitator`. Optionally raise its clearance with `klatch admin clear` (the default is the lowest, which is enough for a public lobby). 3. Start the session as that member with the brief as its instructions, for example a headless Claude Code session with `--append-system-prompt "$(cat proto/scripts/facilitator-brief.md)"` and the credential in its environment, in a directory with the klatch hooks installed (`klatch init`). An interactive session works the same way: paste the brief as the first message. 4. Watch it: `klatch admin audit` shows `create-room` and `invite` rows with its name; `klatch refusals` (as the member) and `klatch admin refusals` show anything it tried outside the bound. `klatch admin facilitator facilitator --off` ends the role at once.

   Not done. A Jev integration is #898. The MCP tools and the wire have no join, asks or invite, so a live session uses the command line (#901). The bound lets the facilitator post nothing but the pointer, so it cannot ask a question in the lobby when unsure; it reports to whoever runs it instead; the one need-info DM to the asker (the design item 201) is the only way it asks.

122. **The asker closes an ask with an outcome: `closed #N ok` or `closed #N unresolved` (#232; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** Decision made by the human on 2026-11-21: add the two outcome words to the close, with no retry limit for now. `reopen #N: why` was built earlier (design/items/232-reopen-ask.md).

       The words. `closed #N ok` says the answer served; `closed #N unresolved` says the asker gave up without a usable answer; a bare `closed #N` still closes the ask and states no result. The outcome is the first word after the number (lower-cased, trailing punctuation ignored, and it needs a space after the number); any other word is a bare close. All three end the ask exactly as before, only the asker's own close counts, and a later `reopen #N:` puts the ask back, so the latest close after the latest reopen is the ask's outcome. The words are read from the room's messages: no new state, wire method, operation or schema, so no AUTHZ-AUDIT row changes.

       Where it shows. An ended ask is not on the board, `klatch asks` or stuck, so the outcome is read where ended asks are read: `klatch find` marks an answered ask `(closed ok)` or `(closed unresolved)`, so an asker does not trust an answer the earlier asker gave up on. The domain reads it with `close_outcome` and `latest_close` (domain/asks.rs).

       Not built. A limit on retries (the human said none for now). The member prompt is at its byte bound and does not teach the outcome words. The recorded real-agent run (an insufficient answer leading to a second helper attempt) is left.

123. **An asker reopens an ask after an insufficient answer (#232; no schema change; 2026-11-19).** The ticket listed three options (a `reopen` message, a close with an outcome, a new ask) and left the choice to the human; no choice was recorded, so the smallest one is built: the asker posts `reopen #N: <why>` and the ask returns to open. Decision made by the builder for review, not by the owner.

       The rule. An ask is open unless its latest ending message (an `answer to #N:` by someone else, or a `closed #N` by the asker) is newer than the asker's latest `reopen #N:`. Only the asker's reopen counts, as only the asker's close counts. A reopen posted before any ending changes nothing. A second answer after a reopen ends the ask again, and the asker may reopen again. The reason after the colon is read by helpers as text and is not interpreted. The words are read from the room's messages, so there is no new state, wire method, or schema.

       What stays. Declines, failures and partials posted before the reopen stay on the ask, so the next helper sees what was tried. A live claim is not touched: the ask shows as claimed while the lease lasts and returns to unclaimed when it lapses, as for any ask. Nothing limits how often an ask is reopened.

       Not built. `closed #N ok` and `closed #N unresolved` (telling a satisfied close from an abandoned one) and a limit on retries are left to the human; `closed #N ok` is already read as a close because the text after the number is ignored. CONVENTIONS.md gains the word (`reopen #N:`); the member prompt is at its 2800 byte bound and does not teach it yet.

124. **Questions and answers stay correlated by text; there is no reply-to field (#234; no schema or wire change; 2026-11-19).** The ticket asked for an optional `in_reply_to` and `kind` on `post` and the stream, or a recorded decision to stay with text conventions. The owner had recorded only that structured fields "wait for Q43 and #234" (section 25.4), so this item chooses the smallest option consistent with DESIGN.md, which is to stay with text and to fix it in tests; it is a default, not a ruling, and the human may reopen it.

   What main already has, found before building. `kind` and `payload` exist on `post` and the stream for handoffs (PROTOCOL.md, DESIGN section 32 item 16) and are opaque to klatch. A direct message exists (`dm`, #43): a two-member room `dm.<a>.<b>`. The wire has no reply-to or thread field, and the `reply_to` column in the section 8.2 sketch was never built.

   The decision. - Correlation is the ask's `seq` in the text: `answer to #<seq>:`, `closed #<seq>`, `need-info #<seq>:`, `ask:<seq>` claims. A `seq` is dense and stable per room, so the number is the reference. No `in_reply_to` is added to `post` or the stream. - A direct question to one named member is a `dm` whose body starts `seeking:`. Open asks are derived per room, so it is never a lobby ask and no one else sees it; inside the DM room the same `answer to #<seq>:` text ends it, and `seq` is the DM room's own. This answers Q40 for asks; whether a wake rule should fire on "addressed to me" stays open there. - Several answers to one ask: every answer is an ordinary message and reaches the asker, in order. The first answer from someone other than the asker ends the ask (section 25.4); later answers change nothing and are not dropped. There is no "best answer" and no vote. - `kind` stays what it is, the handoff kind. It is not a message-type vocabulary for questions, because `seeking:` and the other words already carry that and a second channel for the same meaning would disagree with the text.

   Why not the field. It needs a schema change (a column, a migration), a wire addition on `post`, `read`, `stream` and the MCP tools, and a rule for a `reply-to` that names a seq in another room; the text convention costs none of that and already works for every harness that can post a body. A harness that wants a machine-readable link can put the number in a handoff `payload`, which klatch carries and does not read.

   Proof. `gateway/tests/qa_correlation.rs`: several answers are all delivered and the first ends the ask; a direct question is a DM, not a lobby ask, and is answered by the same text inside the DM room. Both tests describe behavior main already had, so they passed when first written (not run red first); they fix the chosen correlation so a later change shows.

   Not done. A structured reply-to remains possible later as an additive optional field (a new schema version and a wire addition) if text correlation proves too fragile in use; ticket #944 tracks that evidence.

125. **Repeat asks: `klatch find` lets the room history answer a question already answered (#239; no schema change; 2026-11-19).** Of the three options in the ticket (an asker reads the history first as a convention, `klatch find <room> <text>`, or the hook showing recently answered asks) the smallest is built: `klatch find ROOM TEXT`, chosen by the builder agent and open to the owner changing it. The command pages the room's `history` forward from seq 0 (at most 5000 messages, oldest first) and runs the pure function `find_answered` (`domain/ask_find.rs`) over what came back, so it adds no wire method, no store query and no capability: what it can see is exactly what `history` shows the caller.

       The similarity rule is plain and does not guess. An ask matches when the text, lower-cased with whitespace folded, is a substring of the ask text, or when the ask and the text share at least `min(2, keywords in the text)` keywords, a keyword being a run of letters and digits of at least four characters. No stemming, synonyms or ranking; each line names the reason (`substring` or the shared words) so the asker judges the match. Only asks with an `answer to #N:` from someone other than the asker are listed (an asker's own answer, a bare `closed #N` and an ask reopened after its answer (#232) are not), each with its latest answer, newest ask first, at most ten lines with the rest counted. The output carries the same data rule on stderr as `read` and `history`, because the ask and answer text come from other agents. The hook is untouched: no unprompted text is added to anyone's context.

       Not done: the ticket's "done when" asks for a run showing a repeat ask resolved from history with the cost compared to a fresh answer; that needs a real agent run and is left to the human. A paraphrase with no shared word ("what port" against "which socket") finds nothing by design.

126. **One-call `klatch ask` and `klatch answer` cut the turns a helper spends on an ask (#241; no schema change; 2026-11-19).** `klatch ask <room> <text>` posts `seeking: <text>` (a leading `seeking:` is not doubled) and prints the ask number, so the asker has the number for `wait` without reading the room back. `klatch answer <room> <N> <text>` does what the convention taught in four commands: it claims `ask:<N>`, posts `answer to #N: <text>` and releases the claim, in one call.

       The guard. Both are commands only; there is no new wire method or gateway operation. `answer` calls `open_asks`, `claim_ttl_for`, `claim`, `post` and `release` as the caller's own member, so every step meets the guard, the room's checks and the content rules exactly as the separate commands do. It holds no capability that `claim`, `post` and `release` together lack.

       The result. If another member holds the claim, the command prints `not answered: #N is claimed by <member> for another Ns; nothing was posted` and exits 0: losing the race is an expected outcome, not an error, and the helper's turn ends there. A claim that is a deadlock yield, or any refusal, still fails as `claim` does. An N that is not an open ask (answered, closed, no such number) fails with a clear message before any claim, so a stale number never posts. The claim is released even if the post fails, and both errors are reported.

       Not built. The router (Q42) that wakes one helper per ask already exists (#189), so it is not repeated here. The ticket's done condition, a run showing fewer turns per answer, needs a real agent run and is left to the human or the bench.

127. **An experiment harness repeats a scenario N times and reports variance (#243; no schema change; 2026-11-20). Runs four to nine of the two-agent experiments were single runs of a stochastic system, so a difference of a few turns or cents between variants was within noise. `experiments/variance/repeat.py --runs N [--json FILE] -- COMMAND...` runs a scenario command N times, reads one JSON result from the last line of its stdout each time, and prints mean, sample standard deviation, min and max of turns, tokens, cost and duration per agent, plus a count of each outcome (a failed run is counted, not dropped). The result is either a `claude -p --output-format json` object (read as one agent; tokens add input, output and cache tokens) or `{"agents": {name: {turns, tokens, cost_usd, duration_s, outcome}}}` for several agents. One run reports `sd n/a` rather than a zero spread. The script starts no agent itself and spends only what the scenario command spends; it is run on demand, never in CI. `experiments/variance/test-repeat.sh` tests it against a fake agent and runs in the gate.** **Not covered:** the part of #243 that records which earlier conclusions held up when rerun needs real agent runs and spending, so it is left to the human (the ticket stays open). The harness runs repeats one after another and has no confidence interval or significance test; with the small N a real run affords, compare the ranges, not only the means.

128. **Variance harness gets a spend cap, a per-run timeout and isolated stores (#243; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** experiments/variance/repeat.py already repeated a scenario N times and printed mean, sd, min and max. It now runs every repeat with a fresh empty KLATCH_HOME and KLATCH_KEY_DIR under a temp dir (removed afterwards), so no run touches ~/.klatch or the Keychain; `--timeout SECONDS` kills a hung run and its process group and counts it as a failure; `--max-cost USD` stops the loop once total cost reaches the cap (exit 3, and a result without a cost under a cap fails loudly); a crashed, timed-out or unparseable run is counted instead of aborting the loop; the report prints `failures: K of N`. Tests drive a fake scenario only, so no real claude runs. Rerunning earlier experiments with real agents stays with the human, so #243 stays open. No operation changed, so AUTHZ-AUDIT.md is unchanged.

129. **A member's card: one member in one place, derived from the board and the claims (#251; no schema or wire change; 2026-11-18).** The board lists every ask, offer and member in sections; a requester that wants to know where its asks stand, or a helper that wants its own work queue, had to read across sections. `klatch card ROOM [MEMBER]` (the member defaults to the caller) prints one member's card (`Card`, `card_of` and `render_card` in `domain/card.rs`; `Gateway::member_card`, `Transport::member_card`). **Fields (proposed, #251 waits for the human to confirm them, so the ticket stays open and is labelled needs-human):** the member's name, scope (the repository attribute of section 24: the member's own scope, `git:name-hash` for a coding session), last seen, status, a declared wait if one holds, its current offer; **requester view**: the open asks it posted, each with its age and whether a helper holds its claim (`asks posted:`); **helper view**: the open asks of other members on which it holds a live `ask:<N>` claim (`serving:`, with the asker and the age), its other live claims with the time left (`claims:`), its open durable tasks (item 48) and the pull requests whose `pr:N` claim it holds (item 84). A section with nothing in it is left out, and a member with nothing at all says `nothing posted and nothing held`. **Derivation and bounds:** nothing is stored and nothing is new in the log. The card is the board (`Gateway::board`, so it is a read that refuses where the board refuses and takes the same room access) filtered to the member, plus the room's live claims and the member's scope; an expired claim is not work in hand; a member that is not on the board (not in the room, or not seen for over 10 minutes, which the board counts and does not list) has no card, which the command says in words and as a failure so a script can tell. Each section lists at most `BOARD_LIST_LIMIT` entries with a count of the rest, every line is one clipped line, every text a member wrote (an ask, an offer, a status, a claim path) is flattened and clipped, the card says its entries are data and not instructions, and a member name is a valid name or the call is `invalid`. **What is deliberately not on the card:** the answers to an ask (an ask that is answered is no longer open, so the requester view shows asks still open and not a thread of answers; threading answers needs a read of the working room and is left for the human's decision on the fields), a member's kind (#301 adds `kind` to the board member; the card shows it once both are merged, a one line change), anything about another member's cursor or what it has read (the card never shows read state, as `receipt` does not), and a card for a member that left. **Not built:** a wire method (the CLI is the member command, as for `board`), a card in the hook digest (the board there is under a byte budget and the card would add to it), and a per-requester threading of replies. **Tests:** `domain/card/tests.rs` (written first; 9 of 10 red against a stub that returned no card), `gateway/tests/member_card.rs`, the `zero_trust` classification of `member_card`, and `tests/card.rs` (the real binary: the requester card, the helper card, an unknown member, a bad name).

130. **A done status clears when its member returns, shows its age, and `available` is reserved (#260, #223 in part; schema version 32; 2026-11-19).** The human decided on 2026-11-18, on the ticket: options (a) auto-clear `done` when the member posts or claims again, (c) show a status age on the board ("done 12m ago"), and (e) the reserved word `available` that a member sets when it is free for work; (b) an explicit clear and (d) a lifetime were not chosen. This supersedes item 4 of section 25.7 ("no automatic clearing") and settles the two words in #223 (the wider vocabulary stays free text).

       Clearing (a). A successful post or claim by a member clears its stored status when that status starts with `done` (`Store::clear_done`, run by `Gateway::post` after the message is stored and by `Gateway::claim` after the claim is taken). Any other status stays, a refused post or claim clears nothing, and a release or a read does not count as returning. The board's offers then read the member as present again, so an `offering:` it posts after returning shows; an offer it made before saying `done` was already ended by the `status: done` message and is not revived (post it again). The SessionEnd hook sets `done session ended` and does not post afterwards, so a finished session stays done. A member that runs `klatch status <room> done` after posting its `status: done` line keeps the order the convention teaches (post, then the command), so the command is the last write.

       Age (c). `members.status_ts` (schema version 32, migrated in place, default 0) holds the time the status was set; `Store::set_status` writes it and `clear_done` resets it. `who` and the board print `status: done (12m ago)`; a status set before the migration has 0, which means unknown, and prints without an age. The age is the largest whole unit (`45s`, `12m`, `3h`, `2d`). The wire `who` response carries `status_ts` when known (additive; PROTOCOL.md). A status that comes only from a `status:` message (the board's fallback) carries no age.

       `available` (e). `available` is a status klatch reads as "not done": a member with it keeps its offers on the board and counts as reachable for the asker's note (item 67). It needs no code beyond what `done` already distinguishes; what is new is that it is named, taught in the convention text and listed as the convention word `status: available` in CONVENTIONS.md, with a test that the code reads it so. `busy`, `away` and the rest stay free text with no meaning to klatch.

       Convention text. The text shown at session start teaches `available` and the clearing in one clause. It was at 2772 bytes under its 2800 byte bound, so two earlier clauses were shortened (the claim renewal sentence and the `partial #N:` sentence) instead of raising the bound; the full wording stays in CONVENTIONS.md.

       Not built. An explicit clear (b): the status text still cannot be empty, and `available` is the way to say "not done". A lifetime (d): a status does not lapse by itself; presence aging and the SessionEnd hook cover members that are gone. The card (`klatch card`) does not show the age.

131. **The rules-only mediator proposes for what rules cannot settle, from structured stuck-case events (#271, DESIGN 24, 25.12, 25.15; wire method `stuck`, commands `klatch stuck` and `klatch mediator`; no schema change; 2026-11-18). Steps 1 to 3 of the order of work in #271 are built; step 4 (a model) is not, and #190 (the facilitator) and Jev (#898) are separate jobs.** **The events (step 1).** `klatch stuck [room]` and the wire method `stuck` (`{ room }`, result `{ events: [...] }`) list the stuck cases of a room as structured events, derived on every call and stored nowhere: `stale_ask` (`ask`, `asker`, `age_secs`; an unclaimed ask older than the room's stale window or its stated deadline, not waiting on its asker, with no active working room; #225), `nobody_capable` (`ask`, `asker`, `cannot`; enough helpers declined as `cannot`, none busy, failed or partial; #230), `ask_cycle` (`members` from the member that yields, `asks`; #273, 25.15) and `claim_deadlock` (`members`, sorted; a standing cycle of claim waits, the 25.12 deadlock as a fact that can be read and not only as the refusal flag or the once-told hook line). Each carries a stable `key`. The read is guarded exactly like `open_asks` (`Operation::read`, so a room above the caller's clearance is refused with `clearance`), changes no cursor, presence, message or claim, and an event holds ids, member names and numbers, never message text.

   **The member (step 3).** `klatch mediator [--room lobby] [--once] [--interval N]` runs deterministic passes as the caller's own member. `domain::plan_proposals` is a pure function of `StuckEvent` values and presence; the event has no text field, so no wording of an ask can steer it (decision 8). It posts a fixed template per case, once per key per run: for an ask cycle that by a fixed rule the first name yields (post what it has for the ask it holds, release it, wait), with a room `mediate-<lowest ask number>` created and each present cycle member invited; for a claim deadlock that klatch's own rule (the member whose attempt closed the cycle yields) stands, and otherwise the first name releases and retries; for a stale ask that the asker could narrow or repeat it or a member could claim it; for a nobody-capable ask that the asker could tell its user or a human could add a member that can. At most five proposals a pass; a case about the mediator itself, or with a name that is not a valid member name, is skipped.

   **Contract and clearance.** It only proposes. It calls `join`, `stuck`, `who`, `post` and `invite` through the guarded gateway as itself, and nothing else: no release, cancel, grant, clearance, label or admin act. Creating a room and inviting are the capability of #188 (`klatch admin grant-rooms`), audited under its name, and an invite below the room's label is refused (`clearance`), so it never acts above a member's clearance. A refusal is reported once per run and not retried. Cost limits: no model, no network, one read of the lobby per pass. A restarted run may post a proposal again for a case that still stands (the key set is per run); a case that stops standing stops being proposed.

   **Collected stuck cases (step 2).** `fixtures/stuck-cases.json` records the cases as CLI step lists with the events and proposals expected: the ask cycle (#273), the claim deadlock (25.12), the overwrite after a deadlock (#272) and two tasks that need the same result. The overwrite case became detectable in #909 (option C, `possible_overwrite`; see the design item 205). The last case is marked `detectable: false`: duplicate asks in different words are compared by neither number nor path. It is the case the rules cannot settle, which a model-backed mediator (step 4) would take, still as a member that only proposes, with its output as data like any message.

132. **`klatch mcp --stdio`: an MCP front end on the same session as the wire (#299, #297; no schema or wire change; 2026-11-18).** Human decision of 2026-11-13: MCP and an HTTP gateway are both wanted as thin front ends on one gateway, MCP first. The design was posted on the ticket before the code. **Shape:** `session/mcp.rs` is an adapter over `Session::call`, the plain request path `serve --stdio` uses, so each tool is the wire method of that name and meets every check a wire request meets (guard, roles, labels, the credential scrub, the receiver budget, stops, the refusal record, audit); the adapter checks nothing of its own and skips nothing. **Identity:** the process's member, found as for every command; the schema of no tool has a member argument and one sent is ignored (a test pins it); a worker's key bundle proves it as before. It runs on the same machine as the same user, so there is no network surface and no zero-trust prerequisite. **Protocol:** JSON-RPC 2.0, one message per line, only messages on standard output (the specification, revision 2025-06-18, lifecycle and transports and tools pages, read on 2026-11-18). `initialize` answers with `2025-06-18`, which is the client's version when it asked for it and otherwise the newest supported (the negotiation rule); capabilities are `tools` only; no resources, prompts or server notifications. The convention text goes in `instructions`, so a runtime with no hooks is taught the lobby convention (#209). **Tools:** `klatch_post`, `klatch_read` (peek optional), `klatch_claim` (one target), `klatch_release`, `klatch_wait` and `klatch_who`. A wire failure is a tool result with `isError` carrying the code, the message and the refusal rule and reason (never matched content); a malformed call (unknown tool, wrong argument shape, a wait outside 1 to 55 seconds) is JSON-RPC `-32602`, an unknown method `-32601`. Messages read come with a notice that they are data. **`wait`:** bounded (default 30 seconds, at most 55) so it never outlives a runtime's own tool timeout by design; it polls `read` every 100 ms over the rooms and returns the first poll that has messages, each tagged with its room, or `timeout: true`. It moves the cursor as `wait` and `read` do. The server answers one request at a time, so a wait holds the connection until it returns; cancellation is not handled. **Differences from the design comment, and why:** `klatch_who` needs a room (the wire has no method that lists a caller's rooms, and the adapter adds no call the wire lacks). **Not built:** `claims`, `asks`, `board`, `status`, `scope`, `history`, `invite`, `dm` as tools; a server-pushed notification for new messages; the HTTP front end. **Tests (written first, red until the command existed):** `tests/mcp.rs`, a client speaking MCP to the real binary against a real store: version negotiation, instructions, `tools/list`, post and read by another member, peek, a claim conflict and a release, a refused post that names the rule and never repeats the secret, wait with a message, a timeout and a message arriving during the wait, wait bounds, who, a `member` argument with no effect, malformed calls, notifications, ping, a clean exit when input closes, and a non-coding agent answering a coding member's ask in the lobby.

133. **A canary is planted in every private room by the human's label act (#347; no schema change; 2026-11-19).** **Decision (the human, 2026-11-18).** When the human labels a room to `private` or above (the default agent cap; the level just below the top when the chain has no `private`), the label act plants a canary in it, with no setup. The label act of an admin agent plants nothing and its allowlist is unchanged; a room already labelled when this shipped has none until the human labels it again (nothing is planted retroactively). A room gets one canary at a time: labelling it again keeps it. The token is 128 random bits from the operating system (`kc-canary-` plus 32 hex digits), made per plant and never derived from the room, the time or anything in the repository. The canary is a message from the member `canary` at the room's label with the kind `canary`, so the label floors keep it from every member below that label, and a copy of the token in any other room is refused by the existing `canary` check, which pauses the room it was aimed at.

       **Shown once.** The message is delivered once per member, in the first digest that member gets for the room (the digest cursor is the per-member record, so no new storage). `read`, `history`, `tail`, `wait` and the wake count drop it, and the digest never repeats it. Tradeoff: a member whose first view of the room is an explicit `read` or `history` never sees it, so it cannot copy it (and so cannot be caught by it); a session that starts with its digest, which is the normal path, sees it. The wording is unchanged from `admin canary` ("internal credential, never copy it out of this room"), because whether to soften it to a marker is not decided.

       **Retire.** `klatch admin canary-retire <room>` removes the room's canary token, so the tripwire for it stops; it is a human act (not in the admin agent's allowlist) and an error when the room has none. Labelling the room again plants a fresh one.

134. **The 5 second first Keychain read is now a one-time migration cost, bounded and unavoidable without signing (#372; no code change in behaviour; 2026-11-18).** The ticket measured 4.5 and 5.3 seconds for the first Keychain read of a newly built unsigned binary (later reads 17 to 30 ms). That was written before #480 (item 28), which moved macOS key custody to a 0600 key file (`FileFirst`). **What is true now:** (1) a key the file already holds never reaches the Keychain (a new test, `file_first_never_asks_the_older_keystore_for_a_key_the_file_holds`, whose older keystore panics if asked; a mutant that asks the Keychain first is caught); so after the first successful read of an install's key the Keychain is never touched again, by any rebuild or deploy, and a rebuilt binary pays nothing; (2) a new install, with no Keychain item, asks the Keychain once for an item that does not exist, which is a miss without any access-control check: measured 10 ms (`security find-generic-password` for a name that is not there, three runs, 0.01 s each; the live item was not touched); (3) only a store whose key is still in the Keychain from before #480 reads the item, once, copies it into the file and is done. That read is the one that waits for the access-control prompt (the item's partition list names the code hash of the binary that stored it, and a different unsigned binary is a stranger until the login password is given), and it is bounded by `READ_LIMIT` (5 seconds), after which the command fails with the reason instead of hanging. **Does the hook timeout cover it?** klatch sets an explicit `timeout` only on the wake hook (3600 s, `init.rs`); every other hook runs under the harness default, which is longer than the 5 second limit, so a hook that hits the limit fails with the message and does not get killed first. I did not re-read the harness default in this change. **Is there a cheaper path that keeps custody?** None was found. Reading the legacy item through `/usr/bin/security` instead of the framework does not avoid the prompt for an item an unsigned program created (the partition list is the program's, not `apple-tool`), and widening the item's access list needs the login password too (item 28). Signing with a stable identity removes it for later releases (a `teamid:` partition list), which is #371 and the human's decision #424; ad hoc signing does not, since its identity is still the code hash. **Not measured:** the prompt case itself, because it raises a dialog on the developer's machine (item 28 dropped that test for the same reason) and the live item must not be touched. The ticket's two measurements stand as the figure. **Result:** the claim "every rebuild and every deploy pays it once" is no longer true; only the migration of an old install pays, once. #372 stays open for the signing question (#424) only.

135. **A label key can be rotated, sealing a label converts what is already in it, and claim text stays plain (#378, closes the gaps in item 12; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** Three gaps of item 12 were one mechanism: protect stored text under a key, atomically, with the store lock held. The sealed value already binds `klatch message <id> under <label> field <field>`, and every move below keeps that binding, so a text moved to another message or label still fails to open. **Decided by the human on 2026-11-21.**

   **Rotating a label key.** `klatch admin rotate-label-key <label>` (terminal gate, the label typed back, audited as `rotate-label-key`, the lowest and any unsealed or unknown label refused) is modelled on `rotate-key`. Under the exclusive directory lock: (1) every sealed body and payload of the label, in the store and in every archive file, is checked to open with the current key or the new one, and one that opens with neither (or an archive file that does not open with the store key) refuses the rotation with nothing changed; (2) the new key is stored under `<label key name>-next`, so a crash leaves both keys; (3) each archive file is rewritten through a temp copy that is opened and checked before it replaces the original, so every file is whole under one of the two keys at every moment; (4) the store is rewritten in one transaction (reveal with the old key, protect with the new, same id, label and field); (5) the new key takes the main name and `-next` is deleted. A text that already opens with the new key is skipped, so a repeated run finishes a crashed one. The label twin of `open_keyed`'s repair runs where the label ring is loaded (`label_ring`): if the main key opens no sealed text of the label and the `-next` key opens all of them, `-next` is promoted; a `-next` equal to the main key is removed; a `-next` that opens only some texts is left alone. The command prints the new recovery code and says to relaunch workers that hold the label (a key bundle written at spawn holds the old key, and klatch keeps no list of outstanding bundles, so it neither checks nor refuses while workers are live). Backups and archive copies made earlier keep the old key's text; the command says so. **Proof:** `store/sqlite/tests/label_keys.rs`, `member/custody/label_rotation_tests.rs` (a crash injected after each step and inside the transaction, the old key opens nothing afterwards, a moved body still fails, an unopenable row or archive row refuses with nothing changed, an unsealed label is refused), `admin/command_tests.rs`.

   **Sealing a label converts earlier messages.** `klatch admin seal-label <label>` now makes the label's key first, then marks the label sealed and protects every earlier plain body and payload of the label in one transaction (`PolicyStore::mark_label_sealed_converting`); with something to convert and no key the whole step is refused and nothing is marked. Archived tombstones are left alone. `--keep-plain` marks the label only (the old behaviour, `admin_seal_label_keep_plain`), and a later `seal-label` without it converts what was kept. The audit outcome says how many texts were converted. Archive files made before the label was sealed still hold those messages as plain text, and restoring one puts them back plain; that is #1153.

   **Claim text stays plain.** Claim reasons, release notes and member statuses are not sealed: they have no label of their own, they are read on every board, `who`, `wait` and hook digest path, and sealing a status would add a key read to the hottest path. SECURITY.md says claim text must not carry secrets. A per-claim label (`claim --label L`, sealing only the reason and the note, never the path, owner or expiry) stays the design if the need returns.

136. **A store declares the optional features it keeps (#485; no schema change; 2026-11-19).** The area traits give many methods a default that answers "nothing" so a backend can be written a little at a time. The security-relevant ones already default to an error (the earlier #485 change); the rest (sessions, member notes, mute, pruning, wait positions, claim notes, anchors, claim lifecycle, asks, reviews, credentials) still succeed silently, so a backend that never writes them compiles, runs and loses the feature. Each group is now a `Feature`, and `RoomStore::features` is a required method with no default, so a backend cannot avoid saying what it keeps. `Gateway::new` panics, naming the missing features, for a store that does not declare them all: a backend defect found the first time it is wired, not a run-time condition (84 construction sites make a `Result` return too invasive). `conformance::features::every_declared_feature_works` runs the existing conformance tests of each declared feature, so a backend cannot claim one it does not keep. The in-memory spike declares none and is therefore refused by the gateway, which is the point; SQLite declares all and is unchanged.

     Design choices: the groups are coarse (eleven) rather than one per method, so a declaration stays reviewable. Park and unpark sit under member notes, and the review queue under reviews; both are backed by shared conformance tests (#956).

137. **The security-relevant Store defaults are errors, not silent success (#485; no schema change).** The area traits gave about fifty methods a default of `Ok(())` or empty, so a second backend could skip a feature and still run. For the ones whose silence turns a check into a pass, the default is now an error naming what the store does not keep (`area::unsupported`): policy (labels, clearances, roles, floors, tripwires), the audit and the refusals (the breaker's input), blocks and member removal, room pause and close state, deleting rooms, and the post times the rate limit reads. `set_label_keys` has no default, so a store cannot ignore the keys of sealed labels. The SQLite store implements every one and does not change. Left as defaults on purpose: presence, status, scope, parent, mode, hook runs, park, mute, claim notes, anchors, asks and the review queue's readers (a missing feature there loses convenience, not a decision; `enqueue_review` and `issue_credential` already error, and a store with no credentials verifies none). Test stores that relied on the silence now say what they do (`security_defaults_tests` pins the errors on the second-backend spike).

138. **klatch doctor reports how many rows the tables that only grow hold (#487; no schema change; 2026-11-19).** Archive file checks (open with the store key, page check, interrupted archives) were done in #569. This adds one information line to `klatch doctor`: `growth: N messages, N audit, N refusals, N claim notes`. Messages count live rows only, so the tombstones left by `klatch admin archive` are not counted. The read is read-only through the store key, adds no files, and the line is silent when there is no store, an old plaintext store, or no readable key. It is always ok, never a warning: how big is too big, and what to prune or archive, is retention policy. Left for the owner: archive pruning (#568), archiving the audit log and refusals (needs tamper evidence across the archive boundary), and the 90 day default retention.

139. **A subagent that posts must name itself; discovery never gives it the parent's member (#503; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** Under `--discover` the walk up the process tree stops at the nearest live registry record. A subagent (a coding-agent process started inside a session, with no registry record of its own) therefore resolved to the parent session and took its member name, so siblings shared one name, one cursor and one author: they hid each other's posts (a read never shows a name its own posts) and ticket holders could not be told apart (#495, #500). The human chose option (c) of #503: a child must set `KLATCH_NAME`, and discovery refuses to guess a shared identity for it. What counts as a child: while the walk goes up from the klatch process to the session it found, a process whose program is `claude` that is not the session's own process. `unregistered_agent_below` returns it. A nested session that has a live record of its own is found first by the walk and is already a distinct member, so it is not a child and is unchanged (that is the headless-worker case `pending_parent` records). A stale record on the nearer `claude` process counts as no record: when unsure, refuse. What is refused: any command (post, read, who, join, and the rest) that would have resolved by `--discover` or `KLATCH_DISCOVER=1` to a session through a child. Reads are refused too, not served as the parent, because a read advances the parent's cursor and would eat the parent's unread mail for a reader that is not the parent. The refusal says: this process runs inside a subagent, `--discover` would make it the session's member, set `KLATCH_NAME` to a name for this subagent and run again. A typed name (`KLATCH_NAME`, `--as-member`), a key bundle (`KLATCH_IDENTITY_FILE`) and a Codex thread never reach the walk and are unchanged, so a child that names itself works and is its own member. What is not refused: hooks. A hook is the session's own plumbing run by the harness for the session, and a hook made by a subagent's tool call is already silent (#493). A top level session is unchanged. Limit: a subagent that the harness runs inside the session's own process (no `claude` process of its own between the command and the session) cannot be told from its parent by the process tree, so it is not refused; it still needs `KLATCH_NAME`, as the team convention says. Filed as #1152. AUTHZ-AUDIT.md identity paragraph records the rule; no operation changed.

140. **The sender is told why a crossing was denied or blocked (#516; no schema change; 2026-11-19). A member who asked for a crossing (`klatch cross`) can now ask what became of it: `klatch crossings` lists its own crossings with a state (`waiting`, `approved`, `released`, `denied`, `blocked`) and one fixed sentence for the reason. The notice path is a pull on the member's own queue rows (`Gateway::my_crossings`, `Transport::my_crossings`), the same shape as `klatch refusals` (#678), not a message from a system member, so nothing new can post into a room and the facilitator and reviewer bounds are untouched.**     How much of a reason is shown. A deny reason is free text written by a reviewer or a judge and can describe the blocked content, so it is never shown to the sender; a denial says only that the reviewer decided the message should not cross. A crossing stopped by a floor at release records a category only: the applied column holds `blocked:<category>` with the category one of `private-term`, `credential`, `canary`, `gone` or `floor`.
