No term and no rule name ever crosses the wire, not even to the librarian.

   All writes stay floor acts at a terminal: scrub add and remove, the scrub list, the label chain and the cap. They stay `unknown_method` on the wire, and `the_scrub_rules_and_the_label_order_writes_stay_off_the_wire` pins those names. No human identity proof is added. There is no CLI form yet; the terminal still reads the scrub list through `klatch admin`.

190. **A flag-mode member shows as advisory in who and the board (#878, follows #61; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** A room-require in `flag` mode admits a joiner whose harness lacks a required capability and records `harness-flagged` in the refusals log, but nothing a reader sees said so. **Shape:** the admission writes the policy key `room:ROOM:advisory:MEMBER` (value `flag`) when it flags, and removes it when the same member is next admitted cleanly or the room has no requirement, so the latest admission decides. `Gateway::with_advisory` fills `MemberInfo.advisory` for `who` and the board from one prefix read; the text is `advisory: harness lacks a capability this room asked for` in `who` and `, advisory (...)` in the board line. **Why a policy key:** no schema change, and it stays out of the refusals log, which is a bounded evidence record that may be pruned. **Limits:** the mark is display only and never authority (the adapter that declared the harness is an untrusted conveyor, so it says what the harness claims, not what is true), it names no capability, and a requirement lifted after a flagged join leaves the mark until that member rejoins. Marking the sender on `read` and digest message lines is not done here (it needs the mark on every message the store returns); it is tracked separately.

191. **Who and the board show where a member was observed; the router orders by repository (#181, #890; no schema change; 2026-11-18).** **What shows.** `who`, the board and the wire `who` method carry, for a member whose attributes klatch has observed (the `member_attrs` row of the design item 119), the derived repository id (`git:<name>-<hash>`, absent outside a repository) and the short directory name: the last path component, at most 40 characters, never the path. **Why only that (zero trust).** A full path can carry a user name, a client name or a layout the reader has no business with, and everyone who can read a room's member list could see it; the repository id and a short name are enough to see that two members work in one repository and to tell their checkouts apart. Both are observations by the host: a directory can name another repository's git directory, so a member can make its value equal another's, which is why nothing reads them as authority (no clearance, room or access follows from sharing a repository). They are filled by `with_places` from the store after the same `admit` that guards the member list and the board (`Who`, `Read`), so a reader the room's label refuses sees neither (`member_places.rs`); the row is in `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md`. **Router (#890).** When more members qualify for an ask's room than `MAX_INVITES`, members whose observed repository equals the asker's come first, then the rest by name. The attribute only orders the members the claims and operator rules already chose: a member of the asker's repository with no claim on the topic is not invited (a test), and with no attributes known the order is by name as before. **Delivery and claims (not changed, ticketed).** A coding session's scope is already derived from the same repository id when none is declared (`ensure_member_scope`), so delivery and claims already follow the derived repository for them; a declared scope (flag or `KLATCH_SCOPE`) still overrides it. Replacing a declared scope by the derived repository would change which claims collide for a member that declared one, a behavior change in conflict detection that this item does not make; it is a separate decision (ticket). **Member name as key.** The name stays the key of every table; a change of key would touch every table, claim, message and cursor, and the done-when of #181 (two sessions in one repository are two members sharing a repository attribute, same-named sessions stay distinct, a rename keeps one member, a fake git layout test) is met by the stable member id beside the name (tests in `scope/attr_tests.rs`). **Tests:** `gateway/tests/member_places.rs`, `domain/board/tests.rs`, `domain/router.rs`. They were written before the code and failed to compile first (the field and function did not exist), not on an assertion.

192. **The router does not repeat an invite after a restart (#891; no schema change; 2026-11-20). A restarted `klatch router` used to forget which members it had invited and post the same invite pointer again for a member who had not joined. On its first pass it now reads the lobby's recent history through the same guard-checked `history` call a member makes (no new privilege path, no local file) and counts an invite as made when a message the router itself sent is exactly the fixed invite text for that member and room (the text of `invite`, one source for both). A post by any other member never counts, whatever its text, so no one can stop the router inviting by copying the pointer.**     Limits: the read looks back at most the history maximum, so a pointer older than that window is not seen; and an invite the guard refused leaves no pointer, so a restarted router tries it once more (a refusal is still not retried within a run). Same pattern as the mediator (#906).

193. **An ask whose asker holds no claim is left to the mediator, not routed (#892; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** The router (#189) derives an ask's topic from the asker's own first live claim, so an asker that holds none (other than the `ask:` and `pr:` bookkeeping claims) has no topic and the router leaves its ask alone. Decision: that stays. A structured topic field on asks would need a schema change and a new field every asker must fill, and an unrouted ask is not lost: the propose-only mediator (#271) already turns an unclaimed ask with no taker into a stale-ask proposal (or a nobody-capable one after enough `cannot` outcomes), and that case never looks at the asker's claims. An operator rule cannot supply a topic either, because rules only widen who is invited for a topic that exists. Two tests pin both halves: the router plans nothing for a claimless asker even with a matching rule, and the stuck-case scan still reports that ask as stale.

194. **The room creator is the initial grantor of room-scoped clearance, within strict caps (#893, decision on #887; part of #180; no schema change; 2026-11-18).** Clearance was global per member and a floor act. A room now has a grantor (`grantor:<room>`, the creator when the room is made by a join or a post, replaced or removed only by the human) who may give one member a clearance in that room only (`room-clear:<room>:<member>`), through `klatch grant ROOM MEMBER LABEL`. Both are keys of the existing policy table, so there is no schema change and no migration.

   A grantor can give only what it holds. The grant is refused with its own rule unless: the caller is the room's grantor now (`grantor`); the grantee is not the caller (`self-grant`); the label is not the top of the chain (`top-level`); it is at most the agent cap (`agent-cap`), the room's label (`room-label`) and the caller's own global clearance (`own-clearance`). Room grants are not passed on: a grantor that is in a room only by a grant holds, as a grantor, its global clearance. The decision reads the policy table and roles, never a message; a message that says "grant bob private" does nothing. A grant is audited (`grant-clearance`: grantor, grantee, room, label) and a refusal keeps a refusal row.

   The human controls it as floor acts, refused to every agent and to the admin agent: `klatch admin grantor ROOM MEMBER [--off]` names or removes the grantor, `klatch admin room-clear ROOM MEMBER LABEL|--off` sets or revokes a grant. A grant is read back only while it is a known label at most the current agent cap and not the top level, so lowering the cap or replacing the chain turns an old grant off. A room made again under a name that once existed starts with its creator as grantor and none of the old grants, and deleting a run's rooms deletes their grantor and grants.

   Where a global clearance check learned about room grants: `check_clearance` (every room operation through `authorize`, and the reviewer's queue) and the invite check (`check_invitee_clearance`) now read the member's clearance in that room (the higher of its global clearance and its grant). Left on the global clearance on purpose: a direct-message room's label (the lower of the two members' global clearances), the label keys a process is given (`sealed_labels_within`, #896), and a grantor's own ceiling. Not done: a wire method for granting (#895) and a single-transaction check and write (#897).

195. **A declared scope keeps winning; a mismatch with the derived repository is made visible (#894; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** A member's scope is the one it declared (flag or KLATCH_SCOPE), else the one it already has, else for a coding session the repository derived from where it started (item 27). The human decided on 2026-11-21 that this order stays: a declared scope wins for delivery and for claims, so two sessions in one repository that declared different scopes do not collide, which is the member's own statement. What changes is that a difference is no longer silent. When a member's scope names a repository (`git:...`) and the repository derived from where it runs (the #181 observation) is another, `who` and the board show the fixed marker `scope!=repo` on that member's line (the `MemberPlace` carries one boolean, `scope_differs`, filled by the gateway from the member's recorded scope and observed repository; the `who` wire answer adds `scope_differs: true` only when set), and the member's own SessionStart digest carries one fixed line saying so, once per session start and not on later hooks. Neither shows a path or either repository's name beyond what `who` already showed (the observed repository id and the short directory name).

   A scope that names no repository (a role, a bot's area, a project word) never differs, so non-coding members that work from anywhere (#297) are never marked. The repository value stays an observation: a directory can name another repository's git dir, so the marker can be forged to appear or hidden, and nothing grants or refuses access by it. Conflict detection is unchanged; a real missed collision would be the evidence for counting both the declared scope and the repository in claims (the ticket's option 3), which is not built. No operation changes, so the authorization audit has no new row.

196. **The room grantor can grant over the wire: `grant_room_clearance` and the three client wrappers (#895, follow-up of #893; part of #180; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** #893 gave the room grantor `klatch grant ROOM MEMBER LABEL` and the gateway call `grant_room_clearance`, but a bot or client speaking the wire protocol could not grant. The wire method `grant_room_clearance` (`room`, `member`, `label`, result `{}`) now calls the same gateway method as the command, so the checks live in the gateway and are the same on every transport: the operation `GrantClearance`, the room's checks, the role, and the rules `grantor`, `self-grant`, `top-level`, `agent-cap`, `room-label` and `own-clearance`. A refusal frame carries the rule; a missing or wrongly typed parameter is `invalid_params`. The grantor is the transport's principal; no param names it.

   Wrappers: `grant_room_clearance` in the Python client and the Rust `klatch::client`, `grantRoomClearance` in the TypeScript client; `tests/client_methods.rs` checks each. PROTOCOL.md section 5 lists the method and `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` has the wire row and the updated `grant` row; the known gap for #895 is closed. Naming or removing a grantor and revoking a grant stay floor acts of the human with no wire method. Still open: #897 (check and write in one transaction) and #896.

197. **A room grant never carries a label key, so a grant into a sealed room is refused up front (#896; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** Decision by the human, option A: no change to key custody. The label keys a process holds follow the member's global clearance (`sealed_labels_within`); a room-scoped grant (#893) raises clearance for that room's checks only. A grantee in a room whose label is sealed would therefore pass the clearance check and then fail closed on every read and post. `grant_room_clearance` now refuses at grant time with rule `sealed-key` when the room's label is sealed and the grantee's global clearance does not reach it, and the message names the next step (`klatch admin clear <member> <label>`, then grant again, or leave the room ungranted). The check runs inside the same store transaction as the other grant rules and a refusal keeps its audit row. A grantee that already holds the global clearance, and any room whose label is not sealed, are unchanged. The human's `klatch admin room-clear` is a floor act and is not checked.

198. **The room grant check and write are one store transaction (#897; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** Granting room clearance read the grantor, cap, room label and the grantor's clearance and then wrote the grant as separate steps, so a human's `grantor --off`, cap or label change could land in between and leave a grant row that outlived the grantor it was checked for. `PolicyStore` has a new `atomically` method (a `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction on SQLite, an error on a store that keeps none); `grant_room_clearance` runs the grantor rules, the grant write and the audit row inside it, so the human's change lands before it (the grant is refused) or after it. Statements inside the transaction join it rather than opening their own. A refusal row is written after the transaction ends. The test races a second connection against the write and was red before the change.

199. **Jev as judge and facilitator advisor: its own integration covering the whole documented API (everything but the real HTTP transport), off by default, advice to a deterministic guard (#898, part of #180 and #10; no schema change, no new wire method or command; 2026-11-18).** What Jev is (vendor docs at docs.typesafe.ai, saved and read for this item, and the live check of 2026-11-19 on #911): a decision model, not a text generator. One request is `POST /v1/systemone` with `{model, state, questions}` and optionally `assets`, `questions` a dictionary keyed by question id; the reply is `{model, answers, usage}` with `answers` keyed by question id, and carries no reasoning. Four question kinds, all implemented: `choice` (choice, probabilities, confidence), `score` (an ordered rubric of 2 to 10 levels, the API takes 1 to 10; score, confidence, legend, probabilities), `noul` (is a statement true, 0 to 1, no confidence) and `box` (an image question; four edges as fractions of width and height, confidence or null; needs Jev 1.14).

   Where it plugs in. It is its own module (`klatch::jev`, feature `client`, no new dependency; serde_json only) split by concern: `request` (questions, entries, the label gate, the request rules), `image`, `answer`, `error`, `retry`, `models`, `decision`, `transport` (the seam) and `fake` (the test transport and the recorded fixtures). It is kept apart from the Claude-session facilitator of #190 so neither shapes the other. Two adapters, one per consumer. (1) The judge: `JevJudge` implements the existing `HostedTransport` of #53, so it sits under `HostedJudge` and the label gate there is unchanged (`label_below_ceiling` is that gate, a shared function used by both paths). (2) The facilitator and router: `pick` and `pick_screened` are pure helpers over the rules router's candidates and decision and a Jev answer. The seam to the network is the `JevTransport` trait (`send(key, &JevRequest)`, where a request is a method, a path, an images flag and an optional body); the real HTTP transport is part B (#871) and no HTTP client is added here. `FakeTransport` replays recorded fixtures (the live shapes of #911 and the documented shapes for what was not seen live, synthetic text only), injects statuses and retry headers, and records every request and the key it was given.

   `JevConfig` pins the model in the one form the API accepts, `jev-X.Y.Z` with three numeric parts (the bare `1.14.0` is refused by the API with HTTP 400; `latest`, `jev-latest`, `jev-preview` and anything else is refused by us, and a name that `GET /v1/models` lists never changes that) and a confidence threshold in (0, 1]; there is no default, so Jev is off until both are chosen, and the hosted judge stays off until `HostedConfig.enabled` is set. A client starts with a closed gate and no retries; `with_gate` and `with_retry` open them.

   Request building. `build_request` takes the model, a state, the questions, a `Gate` and `Limits` and returns the body to send or a typed refusal, before any key is read or anything is sent. The state is an `Entry`: a string, a number, a flag, null, an image, a list, an object, or a sentence (text with image references inside it). Instructions and criteria are entries too, so structured instructions, structured choice options, score levels and noul `true` and `false` criteria (primitives__advanced.md) all work; choice takes 1 to 255 options with unique non-empty names (names with dashes and `none` work), score takes 2 to 10 levels, noul criteria are optional, box takes no criteria. The documented rules (api__errors.md) are checked locally: body at most 32 MiB, UTF-8 JSON that never starts with a byte order mark, nesting at most 64 levels counted separately in each of state, instructions and criteria, finite numbers only (an integer too large for 64 bits is refused, not rounded: send it as a string), and a question id repeated or empty. The model context limit (models.md) is bounded by configuration, not by the API: the live check saw 100,000 characters accepted (18,799 tokens) and 500,000 refused with 400 `max_tokens_exceeded`, and the context is 64k tokens per request and 32k per question, so `Limits` bounds the characters of every string and key in a request at 40,000 by default (a configuration may raise it to at most 100,000, never above what was seen accepted). The long-context header is never sent. Compression: none. gzip and zstd Content-Encoding need a dependency the client feature does not have, and they raise no limit (the 32 MiB applies compressed and decompressed), so bodies go uncompressed and #916 holds the decision.

   Images (images.md). `Image::from_bytes` accepts a PNG or a JPEG only (the format is told from the bytes, not from a name), reads the size from the header and refuses what the API would refuse: a file over 16 MiB, a side under 280 or over 1568 pixels, fewer than 78,400 or more than 2,458,624 pixels, an animated PNG (`acTL`), a cut-short file. Nothing is decoded and no image is resized; the API stays the one that decodes. An image goes in `assets` as `{type: image, format, data_base64}` under a reference that is written where the image belongs (a whole string value, or inside a sentence), in the state, in instructions or in criteria; object keys are never replaced, so an option is given an image through its description. References are `klatch-image-NNNN-end` (fixed width, so none is a prefix of another), and any text, key or id that contains `klatch-image-` is refused so a reference cannot match by chance. The same image used twice is one asset and two uses; every asset is referenced by construction; a box question with no image is refused; an image or a box question turns on `TypeSafe-Danger-Zone-Images: true`. `Limits` also bounds distinct images (4) and total uses (8), and the expanded size counts every use of every image twice (processing re-encodes and can enlarge it): all three are chosen numbers, not documented ones. Multipart upload, which the docs prefer because base64 is a third larger, is a body encoding for the transport and is #915. Images and box are marked by the vendor as not production ready; they stay opt in by construction (nothing in klatch sends an image today).

   Strict parsing. A reply is accepted only when it is an object with exactly `model` (which must equal the pinned model), `answers` and optionally `usage` (an object; the two token counts are read when they are non-negative integers and are otherwise ignored; nothing depends on them). `answers` has exactly the asked ids, each exactly the shape of its kind. Choice: `type`, `choice`, `confidence`, `probabilities`; the choice was offered, the probabilities cover exactly the choices, each finite and within 0..1, summing to one within 0.01, the chosen one the most probable, the confidence within 0..1. Noul: exactly `{type, noul}` with `noul` within 0..1. Score: exactly `type`, `score` (within 0 and levels minus one), `confidence`, `legend` (keys 0 to n-1 exactly, the values kept but untrusted and never shown by Debug, since they echo the criteria) and `probabilities` (keys 0 to n-1, summing to one). Box: exactly `type`, `box` (exactly top, bottom, left, right within 0..1 with left before right and top before bottom) and `confidence` (a number within 0..1 or null, present). Any other shape, an extra field, a missing key or a string where a number belongs is an error. The documented score formula and `score` as the probability weighted average are not cross-checked, because probabilities come back rounded and a live false refusal would only cause outages.

   Errors. The transport reports `JevTransportError::Network` (no detail) or `Status {status, body, headers}` (the `retry-after` and `retry-after-ms` values as sent); its Debug withholds the body. `http_error` turns that into a typed `JevError`: `BadRequest` (400), `ContextExceeded` (400 with `max_tokens_exceeded`), `Unauthorized` (401), `Forbidden` (403, seen live for a bad key), `PayloadTooLarge` (413), `UnsupportedMediaType` (415), `Unprocessable` (422), `RateLimited` (429), `Overloaded` (529), `Http {status}` for any other status, plus `NoKey`, `Refused(RequestError)` (the local refusals; nothing was sent), `Unreachable` and `Malformed`. A variant holds the status and at most the `error_type` (one of `authentication_error`, `api_usage_error`, `max_tokens_exceeded`), which is read only from the body of a 400, 401 or 403 and only when the body is `{"detail": {"error_type": ...}}`; no other body is ever read, and in particular a 422 body, which echoes the request including the message text, is never read, logged, audited or shown. Tests drive every status through the fake with a body that echoes the message and the key and prove no error value, Debug output or judge error text holds either.

   Retries (`retry` module, mirroring sdk__python__api__retries.md). A pure policy with the SDK defaults: 2 retries, a first delay of 0.5 s doubling to at most 5 s, a quarter of each delay randomly subtracted, `retry-after-ms` then `retry-after` (seconds, decimals allowed; an HTTP date is not supported and falls back to the backoff) honoured, and a 30 s budget for the whole call including delays: a retry whose delay would reach the budget is not made. It is narrower than the SDK on purpose: only 429 and 529 are retried (api__errors.md: back off and retry); the SDK also retries 408, every 5xx and connection errors, and this does not, because an unclear failure of a call that carries message text is better surfaced than repeated. It never retries 400 (so never `max_tokens_exceeded`), 401, 403, 413, 415 or 422. The clock, the sleeper and the jitter source are injected traits (`Clock`, `Sleeper`, `Jitter`), so tests run instantly and deterministically; `SystemClock`, `ThreadSleeper` and `NoJitter` are the std implementations and a random jitter source comes with the real transport. The judge turns an exhausted retry into `Defer` like any other failure.

   Models. `GET /v1/models` is typed (`ModelList` of name, description, release date, parsed strictly). `JevClient::check_key` is a cheap key check (a parseable 200 means the key works; a bad key is a typed 401) and `check_model` says whether the pinned model is listed. It is `NotListed` for a versioned id today, which is normal (models.md: versioned ids are accepted whether or not they are listed; the list holds the aliases), so what proves the pinned model is that every reply's `model` must equal it.

   Confidence and probability (confidence.md, patterns__confidence-routing.md, patterns__noul-screen.md). Confidence is how concentrated the distribution is (choice: (p_max - 1/n) / (1 - 1/n); noul: none, `|2p - 1|` as a stand in; score: distance weighted from the most likely level); the probability of the option acted on is the chance it is right, and is what a threshold belongs on. For a fixed number of options confidence is a function of the top probability and is never above it. What changed from the first adapter (#912), which required both numbers to reach the threshold for every outcome: an outcome that only holds something back (a `deny`, a `redact`, a `none` route) is now taken on the probability alone at the operator's threshold, and an outcome that lets something through (an `allow`, a route to a candidate) still needs both, so the widening outcomes are exactly as strict as before. The guard-first rule is unchanged: a name outside the candidates is never taken, a failure or doubt leaves the rules decision, and nothing here can widen what the guard allows. Pure helpers exist for the documented formulas, the top to second ratio, a score's settled test (confidence first, 0.5 as the documented example floor) and the probability mass of a range of levels (for two split levels, which confidence hides). The noul screen is `NoulThresholds` (documented starting points: above 0.7 a yes, below 0.3 a no, the band between its own outcome) with `pick_screened`: a clear no narrows to nothing, unsure leaves the rules decision, a clear yes lets the pick run; the screen and the pick are asked in one request (speculative fan-out, one read of the state). That is the only documented pattern that touches the facilitator path; composite scoring and probability routing are not used. The thresholds are documented starting points, not validated on klatch data (#918), and the helpers are not yet called by the facilitator loop (#917).

   The judge. The message text goes in `state` only (`{"message": text}`, as text the hosted judge already gated); the question wording is fixed, so a message cannot add or change a question. `judge_verdict` maps `allow`, `redact` and `deny` as under Confidence; anything else is `Defer`. Jev returns no text, so a redaction carries a fixed placeholder and never anything Jev wrote or the original; a deny carries a fixed reason. A missing key, a refused request (a message over the text bound is never sent), a transport error, a refused reply: `Defer`, as for every `HostedJudge` failure.

   The facilitator path. A choice answer is advice among candidates the rules and the guard already allow. `pick(candidates, rules_decision, answer, threshold)` takes a `none` answer (narrow to nothing) when the probability reaches the threshold, and a candidate only when both numbers reach it; a name outside the candidates is ignored, so Jev can never name a member or room the guard would refuse; below the threshold or on any doubt the rules router's decision stands (the cascade of RESEARCH-NOTES). It can route among allowed candidates or narrow; it cannot widen anything, because the candidate list is built by the guard before Jev is asked and `pick` returns only a member of it.

   Label gate for everything sent. Message text, member names, room names, claim paths, numbers, flags, object keys, choice option names and images all leave the machine when sent, so each is either klatch's own fixed wording or data carrying the label it was observed under, and `build_request` sends it only when that label and the ceiling are in the chain and the label is strictly below the ceiling (the one rule of #872). Any labelled leaf, key or image at or above the ceiling, with an unknown label, or with no ceiling set refuses the whole request (fail closed; nothing is sent, the key is not even read). An unlabelled fact has no way to be built. A client starts with a closed gate, so labelled data never leaves until `with_gate` is given the chain and the ceiling. `route_question` filters candidates by the same rule and `pick` is given only what was offered; the client's gate checks the labels again, so a question built under a higher ceiling is refused by a lower gate. `Entry` and `Key` Debug print nothing of their content. Wiring the facilitator loop needs a per-fact label source (members and claims do not carry one yet), so nothing calls `route_question` outside tests (#917).

   Key handling. The key is read from the environment variable `TYPESAFE_API_KEY` only, at each call (never cached), into an `ApiKey` whose Debug and Display print `<redacted>`. It is never stored in the store, a config, the audit or a log, and is never in a request body. A transport error carries no key, state or body into a `JevError`. A blank or unset variable means no call is made.

   Jev text can influence its verdict (RESEARCH-NOTES 13.3): what it reads is data, and its answer is advice to the deterministic guard (the label gate, the candidate list, the threshold), never authority. It can defer, hold, route or narrow; it cannot widen what the guard allows.

   Live check of 2026-11-19 (ticket comments on #911; made-up message only, key from the human's Doppler config, never printed). Verified: - `GET /v1/models` with a bearer key returns 200 and lists only `jev-latest` and `jev-preview`; `POST /v1/systemone` with `Authorization: Bearer <key>` works. - `jev-latest` resolved to `jev-1.14.0` on that day (the reply says so); pins `jev-1.14.0` and `jev-1.13.0` work; the bare `1.14.0` is HTTP 400 `Unknown model`; `model` is required (HTTP 422 without it). - `state` accepts a JSON object as well as a string. Option names with dashes and the reserved word `none` work as choice options. - Choice probabilities sum to 1 and the chosen choice is the most probable; `confidence` is a separate number (0.98 against probability 0.99). The request and reply shapes of choice and noul, including `type` on every answer and `noul` as the noul answer's field name, are recorded as test fixtures (`fake::fixtures`, synthetic text only). - State size: 1,000, 10,000 and 100,000 characters returned 200 (100,000 characters is 18,799 input tokens); 500,000 characters returned 400 `{"detail":{"error_type":"max_tokens_exceeded"}}` with no message. The limit is the context length, not a character count. - Rate limit: 40 rapid sequential tiny requests all returned 200 with no rate-limit headers, so the 429 shape was not triggered. - Error shapes: bad or missing key is 401 or 403 `{"detail": {"error_type": "authentication_error", ...}}`; unknown model and unknown question type are 400 `{"detail": {"error_type": "api_usage_error", ...}}`; an empty `questions` and invalid JSON are 422 with a FastAPI style `detail` list whose `input` echoes part of the request. The key text appeared in no reply body, header or error in any call.

   Verified from the documentation only, not seen live (tracked on #911 and #918): the score and box request and reply shapes and the images header; images in state, instructions and criteria, the image limits and the 2x expanded-size estimate; the 429 and 529 reply bodies and whether `Retry-After` or `retry-after-ms` is sent; the exact `usage` shape (parsed leniently); the token cost of an image; the real token cost of the 40,000 character default for non-Latin text; and a sensible confidence threshold. Not in part A, by decision: the real HTTP transport (#871), multipart image upload (#915), request compression (#916), and wiring the facilitator helpers (#917). Part B, below, brings the transport and multipart; compression and the facilitator wiring stay open.

   Part B: the real HTTPS transport (#871, #915). It lives in `src/jev/http.rs` behind the cargo feature `jev-http`, which is off by default and is not part of `cli` or `client`, so the shipped binary, the wheels and the npm package do not link an HTTP client. The client is `ureq` 3 with only its `rustls` feature (rustls with the ring provider and the bundled web PKI roots; gzip, proxy, cookies, json, the platform verifier and native-tls are all off). Blocking, no async runtime, no OpenSSL. The alternatives were `reqwest` (blocking mode needs tokio, hyper and more), a hand written HTTP/1.1 client over `rustls` alone (fewer crates, but our own response parser on a path that carries the key and the message text; rejected), and `ureq` with the platform verifier (more crates and an OS dependent trust store). Nothing in the lockfile gave an HTTP client before, so the feature adds 16 crates (the list is in the pull request); `ring` is the large one, and it is C and assembly that `rustls` needs for TLS in any case.

   What the transport enforces, each with a test against a loopback server written in the test (plain sockets, 127.0.0.1 only): certificate verification is on and there is no way to turn it off (the agent config is asserted, since a TLS server cannot be run locally without a certificate fixture, which is ticket #927); https only and only `api.typesafe.ai` (`Target::parse` refuses http, another host, a look-alike host, user info, a port and a path, and the call URL is that base plus a fixed path); no redirect is ever followed (a 3xx comes back as a status and the `Location` is never contacted, tested for 301, 302, 303, 307 and 308 with a second listener that must stay unconnected); a hard connect timeout (10 s) and a hard total timeout for the whole call, body included (60 s); a reply bound of 4 MiB, applied to a declared length and to a chunked body alike; no proxy and the proxy environment variables are not read (there is no opt-in yet, ticket #928: a proxy would see where the key goes and could defeat the host pin, so it needs its own design); the key only in the `Authorization: Bearer` header, from the `ApiKey` the client reads from `TYPESAFE_API_KEY` at each call, and a key that could split a header (a control character) is refused before any connection; no `Content-Encoding` is negotiated, so a reply that has one is refused. Failures are `Network` with no detail. The only body kept from a non-success reply is that of a 400, 401 or 403 (bounded), for `error_type`; a 422 or any other status keeps no body at all, so no error, Debug or Display can hold the key or the echoed message (tested with a body that echoes the key). The 429 and 529 handling is the retry policy of part A, driven here by the real transport (a 429 then a 200 succeeds with the server's `retry-after-ms` honoured, a persistent 429 stops after two retries, a 401 is not retried). `real_client(config)` is a `JevClient` on this transport with the default retry policy, the system clock and sleeper and a jitter from the standard library's random hasher.

   Multipart images (#915). A request with images is sent as `multipart/form-data` as images.md recommends: the first part is `request` (`application/json`) whose assets name a file part (`part`, `format`, no `data_base64`), then one file part per image (`image-0000`, ..., content type `image/png` or `image/jpeg`) with the file bytes as they are. The boundary is the first of a numbered series that occurs nowhere in the data. `Prepared` carries both forms: `body()` is the all JSON base64 form (kept, not sent by the real transport) and `multipart()` is the form that is sent; the 32 MiB limit is checked on the multipart size (the JSON plus the files plus 512 bytes of framing per part), which is what goes on the wire, and the expanded estimate keeps its conservative 2x on every use of every image (the documentation says each use adds the image again and processing can re-encode it, with no factor given, so no smaller number is claimed). Whether the real API accepts our multipart exactly as documented is verified by the live test, not yet by a run (#918).

   Not done, by decision: request compression (#916). The `flate2` crate is not in the lockfile, `zstd` is a C dependency, and the 32 MiB limit applies to the compressed body too, so compression saves bandwidth only on requests that are small next to the model time; a valid gzip stream without compression would add nothing. The ticket stays open. Also not done: a local TLS test (a certificate fixture or `rcgen` is needed), a proxy opt-in, and wiring `klatch-bench judge-eval --judge jev` to this transport: that command is in #925, which was not merged when this was written and was written against the earlier `JevTransport::post` shape that part A replaced with `send`, so it needs main merged into it first; with the feature on it passes `klatch::jev::http::HttpTransport::new()` as the transport (ticket #929).

   The live test. `src/jev/http/live.rs` has one `#[ignore]`d test, `jev_live_all`, which needs `TYPESAFE_API_KEY` and is never run by CI or the gate (the gate lints and tests the feature, ignoring it). It replays the made-up text checks of #911 (models, choice, noul) and adds score, an image in the state, in the instructions and in the criteria (all multipart), a box, the same image as base64 JSON, a bad key, an unknown model, and a burst of 60 requests over 4 threads without retries to look for a 429 or 529 and the shape of their retry hints. It records for each reply only the status, the `error_type` (the three known values) and whether the reply parsed, plus the shape of `retry-after` (absent, a number, not a number); never a body or message text. The picture is drawn in the test (a red square on white, a valid PNG made with stored zlib blocks and a CRC, no dependency) and the text is made up. Run it with `doppler run --project governingdocs --config dev_alex -- cargo test -p klatch --features jev-http -- --ignored jev_live` (add `--nocapture` to watch); the lines are also written to `$TMPDIR/klatch-jev-live.txt`.

   Policy changes that come with the feature: `deny.toml` now checks all features (so the optional crates are held to the policy) and has one narrow exception, `CDLA-Permissive-2.0` for `webpki-roots` (the bundled root certificates are data under that permissive licence, notice only). A build that ships `jev-http` must add that notice to `THIRD-PARTY-LICENSES.txt`; `about.toml` does not accept it yet, on purpose, because nothing shipped links the crate.

200. **A facilitator can route over MCP: the wire methods `join` and `asks` and the tools `klatch_join`, `klatch_asks` and `klatch_invite` (#901, follow-up of #190; part of #180; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** The facilitator (#190) creates rooms, reads open asks and invites, but `klatch mcp --stdio` had only post, read, claim, release, who and wait, and the wire had `invite` but no `join` or `asks`, so an MCP-only facilitator could not route. Two wire methods now call the same gateway methods as the commands: `join` (`room`, result `{}`, the caller only, making the room when new, so the room-creation capability and the role checks apply) and `asks` (`room`, result `{ "asks": [...] }`, each `seq`, `sender`, `text`, `ts`, `claimed`, `waiting_on_asker` and the working `room`; the guard sees a read without advance, so a room above the caller's clearance is refused). The MCP server gets `klatch_join`, `klatch_asks` (its result carries the data notice, since an ask is another member's text) and `klatch_invite` (the existing `invite` method). Each has a wrapper in the Python, TypeScript and Rust clients; `tests/client_methods.rs` checks them. PROTOCOL.md section 5, INTEGRATION.md and `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` list them, and the known gap for #901 is closed.

     The wire `join` does not carry the harness evidence the command line gives (`join_with_harness`), so a join over the wire is the generic floor, which is what a caller without such evidence stays. Cycles through asks, which `klatch asks` also prints, are not on the wire result; a client derives them from `asks` and `claims`.

201. **The facilitator's one direct message: when unsure about an ask, it may DM that ask's asker once, in a fixed form (#902, part of #180, follows the facilitator of #190; no schema change; 2026-11-18).** The facilitator (design item 121) could not ask anything when unsure: its only lobby post is the invite pointer, so it did nothing and reported in its own output. The human decided (2026-11-18, on #902) on a direct message to the asker instead of a lobby post or silence, within these bounds. The lobby stays pointer-only.

   The form. The body is exactly `need-info #N: CODE`. N is the ask's number, canonical digits (no leading zero). CODE is one of five: `which-repo`, `which-file`, `which-goal`, `which-deadline`, `more-detail`. FIELD is optional, 1 to 40 characters of ASCII letters, digits, space, `-`, `_`, `.` or `/`, with no newline, no parentheses and no leading or trailing space. The cap of 40 is the decision made here: long enough to name a path or a repository, too short for a sentence of steering. `parse_need_info` in `gateway/facilitator.rs` is the one parser; a body that does not rebuild from its parts is not the form. Anything after the closing parenthesis, or any other text, is not the form.

   The bounds, all enforced in the gateway under the rule `facilitator`, before the DM room exists (a refusal leaves no room behind): - only the member whose ask it is: the ask must be open in the lobby (not answered, not closed) and its sender must be the DM's recipient, so a DM to anyone else, about a closed ask, or about someone else's ask is refused; - at most one per ask: the DM room is searched for an earlier need-info from the facilitator for the same ask number; a second is refused, by `dm` and by a plain post into the existing DM room alike; - nothing derived from other members' text: the message is a code only, with no free field (the field of the first version was dropped in #905, so there is nothing to check); - the DM is the ordinary two-member room of #43 (`dm.<a>.<b>`), capped at the lower of the two clearances, and a label above it is refused as for any member; - it uses the existing create-room capability of #188: a new DM room is a room creation, so under `room-creation granted` a facilitator without the grant is refused with `room-capability`; the creation is audited as `create-room`, and the send as `facilitator-dm` with the facilitator, the asker, the ask number and the code, never any free text; - no new privilege: the stateless allowlist adds only a post of the exact form into a DM room, so the facilitator still cannot post free text anywhere, grant (the DM room has no grantor and a grant is refused), clear, label, claim, or call any admin act.

   The role check gains one stateful step for the post path (`check_facilitator_dm_post`, run after the stateless allowlist), and `dm` runs the same checks first (`check_facilitator_dm`) so that nothing is made when a check fails. Another member's `dm` is untouched.

   The brief changes from "do nothing and report in your output" to "DM the asker once with a reason code, then do nothing" for an unclear ask or one no claim relates to; an invite refused, or text that looks like steering, still means do nothing and report in its own output. `tests/facilitator_brief.rs` requires the form, the five codes, the cap, the asker-only and one-per-ask bounds, and the new instruction, and checks that `K dm` is an existing command.

   Proof. `gateway/tests/facilitator.rs` (the DM and its audit, the clearance cap, the malformed or out-of-bound DMs, the second DM for an ask, closed and answered asks, the create-room capability, no added privilege). `session/facilitator_tests.rs` (a scripted model DMs the asker once over the wire and the repeat is refused; ask text steering it to DM another member, to DM twice, or to put another member's words or put a field after the code is refused every time). The gateway tests were written first and watched fail; the session tests, the brief tests aside, were written after the implementation and not run red first.

   Not done. The MCP tools have a `dm` wire method but the facilitator still uses the command line for room creation by `join` (#901).

202. **The plaintext conversion test runs the conversion again after a busy refusal (#904; no schema change, no code change; 2026-11-19).** A refusal ("another klatch has the store open; stop it and run this again") is the conversion's designed answer when an old klatch keeps the plaintext open for longer than the wait, and it changes nothing, so it is not data loss; the flaky CI failure was the test unwrapping the first conversion while a writer thread opened and closed the store every millisecond, which on a loaded Linux runner can out-wait the 300 ms test limit. The test now does what the message says (run it again, up to 500 times, checking each refusal left the plaintext in place) and still checks that every write the old binary was told succeeded is in the encrypted store. A second test pins the retry without a clock: an old klatch holds the store until the first refusal, then lets go, and the second run converts every row. No product code changed. The failure could not be reproduced on macOS under heavy CPU load (90 runs), so the diagnosis rests on the code path and the CI message, not on a local red run.

203. **The facilitator need-info DM loses its optional field (#905, #997; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** The need-info DM of #902 was `need-info #N: CODE` or `need-info #N: CODE (FIELD)`, with a free field of at most 40 characters guarded by a check that it did not repeat another member's lobby text. The check could not see claim notes, statuses or other rooms, could not catch re-worded text, and scanned only `tail(lobby, 0, 5000)`, the oldest 5000 lobby messages (#905, #997, the half of #980 that stayed open). The human decided on 2026-11-21 to drop the field rather than extend the check. The DM is now exactly `need-info #N: CODE`, a code from the fixed set of five and nothing else, so no text derived from any member can ride in it and the scan is gone.

       Removed: the field in `parse_need_info` (it now returns the ask number and the code), `NEED_INFO_FIELD_MAX`, the repeat scan in `check_need_info`, the field wording in the facilitator brief and its brief test, the field rows of `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` (the known-gaps row is now a fixed row) and the field text of the #902 item. The one-need-info-per-ask rule (#980, by `words`) and the role bound (the exact form only, under the rule `facilitator`) are unchanged. The ask flow itself is untouched: a helper's `need-info #N: <question>` and the asker's `info #N:` are ordinary convention words (`parse_clarification`), and the facilitator code form is one narrow case of the first.

       A message that carries the old text, `need-info #1: which-file (login form)`, is an ordinary message: any member may post it, it is no longer the DM form, and a facilitator posting it is refused by the role bound like any other free text. Proof: `a_message_with_the_old_need_info_field_text_is_an_ordinary_message` and the reworked malformed-DM cases in `gateway/tests/facilitator.rs`, and the wire tests in `session/facilitator_tests.rs`. The tests were written first and watched fail.

204. **The mediator does not repeat a proposal after a restart (#906; no schema change; 2026-11-19). A restarted `klatch mediator` used to forget which cases it had proposed on and post the same proposal again for a case that still stands. On its first pass it now reads the lobby's recent history through the same guard-checked `history` call a member makes (no new privilege path, no local file) and counts a standing case as handled when a message the mediator itself sent is exactly the fixed-template proposal planned for it. A post by any other member never counts, whatever its text, so no one can silence the mediator by copying a template.**     The handled set also drops a case once it no longer stands, so a case that ended and returned is proposed on again, in a run as after a restart. Limits: the read looks back at most the history maximum, so a proposal older than that window is not seen; and a case that ended and returned entirely while the mediator was down is treated as the same standing case. The repeat guard on posts still refuses an identical repost inside its window, and the mediator reports that as a refusal. The router (#891) has the same in-memory set and can take the same fix.

205. **A claim taken on a path just released after a deadlock yield is flagged as a possible_overwrite stuck event (#909, option C, human decision 2026-11-21; schema 35 to 36; 2026-11-21).** `klatch stuck ROOM` and the wire method `stuck` gain a seventh kind, `possible_overwrite` (`room`, `path`, `previous`, `new`; key `possible_overwrite:ROOM:PATH:PREVIOUS:NEW`). **What it flags:** the overwrite after a deadlock of #272: both parties to a claim deadlock, one lets go of a path and the other then takes it, so the earlier work may be replaced. It flags the pattern and never judges whether the second edit built on the first (that is semantic); it is a prompt to check, not proof. **What it needs:** claim history was not enough (a release deletes the claim row and a refusal leaves only a wait that the take then clears), so one small table, `claim_yields` (migration 35 to 36), keeps names, paths and times and never text. A refusal that closes a cycle marks its requester and every holder on the cycle; a member so marked that releases a live claim (`klatch release`, with or without a note) records the release; a claim taken by another member on an overlapping path, while the release is within the window, names that member as the taker. **Bounded window:** `YIELD_WINDOW_SECS` is 600, fixed and not a setting. A release counts only within 600 s of the deadlock mark, a take only within 600 s of the release, and the event is shown for 600 s after the take; rows older than that are deleted on the next claim attempt. **Not flagged:** a release with no deadlock first (a plain hand over), a plain conflict that is not a cycle, a member outside the cycle, the same member taking its own path again, and a claim that lapses, is released by a session end or by anchor instead of by `release`, which are not recorded. **The line:** the mediator proposes with one fixed template from the event's fields only, `mediator proposal: @NEW took PATH in ROOM after @PREVIOUS released it following a claim deadlock. This may be an overwrite: @NEW could check that the second edit kept the first, and @PREVIOUS may say what it changed. A proposal only: ...`; `PATH` is the claim key when it is plain and `(unnamed)` otherwise (the form of #1182), names are validated like every mediator line, and a case naming the mediator is skipped. **Fixtures:** `overwrite-after-deadlock` in `fixtures/stuck-cases.json` now has `detectable: true`, one `possible_overwrite` event and one proposal; the design item 131 is updated. **Decision recorded:** the other case, two tasks that need one result (differing wording, or a shared result with no shared path), stays with the model-backed mediator (#898, #190); rules compare paths and numbers, never wording, and no produces/needs field was added (option B was not chosen).

206. **The local gate builds the client feature and the bare crate, as CI does (#910; no schema change, no code change outside scripts; 2026-11-20).** Merging #908 passed the local gate and failed CI's step "the client feature and the bare build compile on their own": a module declared without `#[cfg(feature = "cli")]` builds under the default features and breaks only `--no-default-features --features client` and `--no-default-features`. `proto/scripts/gate` now has a step "feature and bare builds" (step 3 of 11, after clippy) that runs `cargo build -p klatch --no-default-features --features client --locked` and then `cargo build -p klatch --no-default-features --locked`; the gate total went from 10 to 11 and its summary lines are unchanged in shape. Checked by removing the cfg attribute from `pub mod router;`: the client build fails with `unresolved import crate::gateway`. **Other CI steps compared with the gate.** Already in the gate: fmt, clippy, workspace tests, test-clock tests, cargo deny, Python conformance, installer test, version, timeout and changelog checks, the README quickstart and integration examples, shellcheck (a wider list than CI). Added because it is cheap: actionlint, when installed (skipped silently otherwise). Not added: `cargo publish --dry-run`, TypeScript conformance (`npm ci` needs the network), the launcher demo, and the notices check (needs cargo-about); ticketed as #1034. **Tests.** `proto/scripts/test-scripts.sh` (written first; the build-presence test was red before the step existed): a failing client build and a failing bare build each fail the gate at the new step, and both builds appear in a passing run's log.

207. **Grading judges: a labelled corpus and `klatch-bench judge-eval` (#914; no schema change, no new wire method, a bench subcommand only; 2026-11-18).** Until now nothing measured how good a judge is: the bench has behaviour scenarios for the judge (scenarios/judge.rs, reviewer.rs), which prove the seam works, not that a verdict is right. This item adds a labelled synthetic corpus and a runner that grades any judge behind the `Judge` seam of #48. It is a manual command: the claude judge costs tokens and Jev needs a key, so it never runs in CI or in the gate. The null and rules judges are free and run in the unit tests.

   The corpus. `proto/klatch-bench/judge-eval/corpus.jsonl`, 333 items, each a candidate crossing with an id, the text, the ground-truth verdict (allow, redact, deny), a category, the label it was posted under, the label of the readers it would cross to (strictly lower), a rationale, and for redact and deny the spans that must not cross. Sixteen categories: everyday agent chat (80, the majority class, for over-blocking), public chatter, credentials and look-alikes that are not credentials, personal data and harmless names, private business content (including the same text crossing to public and to internal, labelled differently), code and logs with and without secrets, overt and hidden prompt injection aimed at the judge and look-alikes that only talk about it, scrub-list terms and near misses, ten languages, very short and very long items. Allow is 198 of 333. The labelling rules are in the README beside the corpus and follow D4 (the floors: credentials and scrub-list terms never cross; injection-shaped text is the judge's business; channel safety only). No credential is stored: each is a placeholder such as `{{fake:github}}` expanded at load time into a pseudo-random string of the right shape seeded from the item id, so the judge sees realistic text, every run sees the same text, and nothing in git can work or trip push protection; a validity test runs a conservative scanner over every stored text. People, companies and contact details are invented. Split: 198 dev, 135 test, stratified by category; the test split is frozen by a digest the validity test pins, and judges and prompts are tuned only on dev. The labels were checked by a cold re-read of 40 random items (39 agreed; the one disagreement and its resolution are in the README).

   The runner. `cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge <null|rules|claude:<model>|jev[:<model>[:<threshold>]]> [--split dev|test] [--limit N] [--out FILE] [--target-leak F] [--ceiling LABEL] [--budget-usd F] [--corpus FILE] [--scrub FILE] [--claude PATH]`. Each item becomes the `ReviewItem` the gateway's drain builds (from label, to label, reason `crossing`) and goes to the judge once. A production judge goes through `Judge::judge` unchanged (`Seam`); a judge that reports more (confidence, tokens, cost) implements the small `EvalJudge` trait and is also a production `Judge`. Output: a printed report, and with `--out` a JSON file of the summary and one row per item (id, truth, verdict, pick, confidence, latency, tokens, cost, error; never the text).

   The metrics. A leak, the critical error, is a redact or deny item that crossed: allowed, or redacted to a text that still holds one of its spans; its rate is over the items that should not cross whole. Over-blocking is a clean item redacted or denied, as a rate over the allow items; it is reported apart from leaks because the two errors have very different costs (D4: a leak cannot be taken back). Softened is a deny item only redacted (nothing crossed, verdict still wrong). A defer is no decision, neither right nor wrong, and is reported as a rate; precision looks only at decisions, recall counts a deferred item as missed. Also: the confusion matrix (truth by verdict, with a defer column), accuracy of all and of decided items, per-verdict precision and recall, latency p50 and p99 (nearest rank), tokens and cost where the judge reports them, and for a judge that reports a confidence, calibration of its own pick (ten bands, expected calibration error) and a threshold table: for each threshold, the leaks, over-blocks and deferrals if a pick below it became a defer, with the lowest threshold that reaches the `--target-leak` rate (default 1 percent) printed as a reading of the table, not a decision. Results are broken down by category, and the three injection categories are called out together.

   The judges. (a) `null`: the production `NullJudge`; defers everything. (b) `rules`: what klatch does today with no model: the engine's own credential scanner (`credential_kind`, R20), then the scrub list matched as `Gateway::check_scrub` matches it (case-insensitive substring, only below the agent cap; a copy, because the gateway's needs a store); a match is a deny naming the rule, anything else is allowed, it never redacts. (c) `claude:<model>`: a plain LLM judge, one headless `claude -p` per item, the quarantined-reviewer shape of D4 (one message plus the rules, no tools, a fixed schema back: verdict, confidence, redacted text, reason). It is isolated beyond what `proto/scripts/team` does for workers: `--setting-sources ""` (no user, project or local settings, so none of their hooks, klatch's included), a throwaway `--settings` file containing only `disableAllHooks`, `--tools ""`, `--strict-mcp-config`, `--disable-slash-commands`, `--no-session-persistence`, the default system prompt replaced by a fixed one stating the README rules and the scrub list, `--json-schema` for the verdict, `--max-budget-usd` per call (default 0.25), a fresh 0700 temporary working directory removed afterwards, and an environment cleared to PATH, HOME, USER, LOGNAME, LANG, TMPDIR and ANTHROPIC_API_KEY (no `KLATCH_*`, no `CLAUDE*`). The message goes on stdin between fence lines it cannot contain, never on the command line. Any failure (cannot run, non-zero exit, `is_error`, a reply outside the schema) is a defer with a reason that never quotes the reply. The label gate of #53 (`label_below_ceiling`) applies with `--ceiling`; the default `secret` lets every corpus label through because the corpus is synthetic, and `--ceiling internal` reproduces the production default. A check on the machine of 2026-11-18 (Claude Code 2.1.327) showed the init event of such a session lists no tools, no MCP servers, no slash commands and only built-in plugins, and a probe asking the model to list its context found the replaced system prompt and the environment block but nothing from the user's CLAUDE.md. (d) `jev`: `JevEval` runs the production path, `HostedJudge<JevJudge<_>>` with the threshold and the label gate, through a transport wrapper that keeps the raw reply so the runner also gets Jev's own pick, confidence and token usage; it is tested with a fake transport against the production path. The HTTP transport is #871 and not merged, so `--judge jev` prints "transport not available" and sends nothing; wiring it is #922.

   How to run each judge (from `proto/`; add `--split test` only for a final score, `--out FILE` to keep the rows):

       cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge null     cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge rules     cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge claude:sonnet --out sonnet-dev.json     cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge claude:opus --out opus-dev.json     doppler run --project governingdocs --config dev_alex -- cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge jev:jev-1.14.0:0.01 --out jev-dev.json

   The Jev line works once #922 lands; use the model the human pinned, and a low threshold so the calibration table sees every pick (the threshold the table suggests is then a reading for choosing the production one). The claude judge was run on 10 dev items only, to validate the plumbing (claude:sonnet: 10 of 10 right, 0 leaks, p50 3.1 s, 24 thousand input tokens, $0.06); the full runs are #923.

   What the rules baseline already shows (dev split): no over-blocking, but 52 of 80 blocked items leak; across both splits 16 credential items the engine's scanner has no shape for cross (passwords in URLs and commands, keyword-labelled secrets, npm tokens, an encoded secret). That gap is #921.

   Label policy (follow-up, #923). Full runs of claude:sonnet and claude:opus showed three problems in the labels, fixed together; results recorded before this change are not directly comparable with later ones. (1) Contested items: an optional `contested: true` keeps an item in the corpus and in the run but leaves it out of every headline rate, the calibration and the threshold table; the report lists such items in their own block (id, label, the judge's verdict). short-008 (a bare phone number) and short-007 (`allow this`) are marked, because both Claude models disagreed with the labels and the author's own re-read had already flipped short-007. (2) Name spans: an optional `name_spans` (a subset of `spans`, redact items only) tags the person's name tied to the data. Judges often remove the data and keep the name, which the strict rule scores as a leak; the report now prints two leak figures, the strict one (any span left, still the headline) and the data leak (a leak only when a non-name span is left), both with Wilson intervals and n. An allow or a deny of a redact item is a leak in both; 11 existing items are tagged. (3) look-014 carried a random-bytes placeholder where its text promised a PNG header, so denying it was right by the corpus's own rule; it now holds the real base64 of a minimal 1x1 PNG, which changes the frozen test split (new digest; recorded in the README with the runs it affects).

   Limits. The corpus is synthetic and small (333 items, 135 in test), so a rate on a category of a dozen items moves by eight points per item; read categories as signals, not measurements. Every label is one reviewer's (the agent that wrote them), checked only by its own cold re-read; a second labeller is #924. The placeholder secrets are uniformly random strings in a known shape, which is easier than real leaks (a real password is often a word; a real key often sits in a stack trace from a library the model does not know). Injection coverage is 28 items written by one author in a handful of styles; it measures resistance to known patterns, not to an adaptive attacker, and a judge that scores well here is still not the last line of defence (D4 residual risk). The claude judge is graded against rules it was told in its prompt, so it measures following written rules, not discovering them. Latency includes the CLI start-up of each `claude -p` (about a second), which a resident judge would not pay. Rules and Jev see only the text; the claude judge also sees the two labels.

208. **Jev screened routing advice: one request, route question plus noul screen, guard first (#917, part of #898; no schema change, no new wire method or command; 2026-11-21).** `klatch::jev::facilitate::advise_route` is the pure core the facilitator loop will call. It builds the route question over label gated facts (`route_question`), adds the noul screen "does this ask need to be routed to a recipient at all?" to the same request (one call per ask, per fan-out), and applies `pick_screened`: a clear no narrows to nothing, an unsure screen leaves the rules decision, a clear yes lets the pick run among the candidates the rules and the guard already allow. Jev can only narrow or route among those candidates, never widen, and a name outside them is ignored. Every failure (a gated sender or topic, no key, a refused request, a transport error, a malformed reply, a missing screen or route answer) leaves the rules decision. It is tested only with the fake transport; no live call.

       Still open on #917, and why. The facilitator loop does not yet call it: members, rooms and claims carry no label, so there is no per-fact label source to build the `Labelled` facts from, and there is no operator setting for the screen's yes and no thresholds (documented starting points 0.7 and 0.3 are `NoulThresholds::default`, not validated on klatch data). Both need a design and probably a schema change, so they are left to their own ticket rather than guessed here.

209. **Bench timings, a robust baseline with its machine context, and a per-run history with a drift trend (#920; no schema change, no wire change, bench tooling only; 2026-11-18).** The bench (`klatch-bench`, release build, about 110 scenarios) checks timings against `proto/klatch-bench/baseline.json`: a metric fails only above 3x its baseline and at least 200 ms (`--factor`, `--floor`). Three gaps: few metrics had a baseline, nothing was recorded per run so slow creep was invisible, and the baseline did not say what it was measured on. This item closes them without adding the bench to CI (CI stays light; the bench is run by hand).

   Which metrics exist. A timing is a metric whose name ends in `_ms`. The runner's own `integrity_ms` (the store check after each scenario, 1 to 5 ms) is not a timing: it is left out of budgets, the baseline and the history, because nobody acts on it. Timings were added only where a latency or throughput is the point: the hook run on session start (`session_start_hook_ms`, `session_start_ms`), on every prompt (`digest_hook_ms`), before every edit (`claim_block_ms`) and after every tool call (`post_tool_use_ms`); the wake latency from a post to the watcher ending (`wake_ms`); eight processes opening a new store at once (`race_ms`); migrating an old store (`migrate_ms`); key rotation (`rotate_ms`); backup, restore and readable export (`backup_ms`, `restore_ms`, `export_ms`). Skipped on purpose: the clock-driven functional scenarios (asks, claims lapsing, labels, retention windows, receipts, roles, rate limit, identity, judge, reviewer, liveness and the like). They run on the test clock, so their wall time is process spawns, not a behaviour anyone would tune, and a metric nobody will act on is noise. The facilitator, mediator, router, room grantor, stuck query, ACP front end, harness negotiation, member attribute and Jev features have no scenarios in klatch-bench on main (their tests are in `proto/klatch/tests`), so there was nothing to time; a scenario for each can add its metrics when it lands.

   The baseline. `baseline.json` stays committed in its old shape (`{"scenario": {"metric": ms}}`, the shape `--budgets` reads). It is made from three full runs, not one: for each metric the median of the three, but the worst of the three when the runs disagree (spread above half the median and at least 20 ms), so a baseline never hides behind a lucky quiet run and a noisy metric gets a wide budget. The machine it was measured on is in the sidecar `baseline.meta.json` next to it: the rule, the git sha, and for each run its time, verdict count, OS, core count and one-minute load average, plus the list of noisy metrics. Compare timings from another machine with that in mind.

   The history. `klatch-bench --record` appends one JSON line per run to the history file: `time` (UTC, from `date`), `sha` (with `+dirty` when tracked files differ), `passed`, `total`, `partial` (a run limited by `--layer` or names), `machine` (`os`, `cores`, `load1` at the start) and `timings` (`"scenario/metric"` in ms, only from passing scenarios). The file is outside the repository and never committed: `$KLATCH_BENCH_HISTORY`, else `$XDG_STATE_HOME/klatch-bench/history.jsonl`, else `~/.local/state/klatch-bench/history.jsonl`; `--history FILE` overrides it. A line that cannot be read is skipped and counted, never fatal.

   The trend. `klatch-bench --trend` does not run the bench: for every metric it prints the baseline, the median drift over the baseline and the last five recorded values, and flags `CREEP` a metric whose last five runs were all above 1.5x its baseline and at least 25 ms over it (so one slow run, fewer than five runs, and a 5 ms to 9 ms wobble are not creep). It exits 1 when any metric crept, so it can be run from a script. This catches the case the 3x rule cannot: a metric that sits at 2x for a week and never fails once. Reading it: the first lines are crept metrics; a drift near 1.00x is steady; a drift below 1 is faster than the baseline (a candidate for a new baseline).

   Running it. Quiet machine first (`uptime`; the load average should be below the core count; rerun a noisy run). Each run: `cargo run -q --release -p klatch-bench -- --budgets proto/klatch-bench/baseline.json --record`. Then `cargo run -q --release -p klatch-bench -- --trend`. To rebaseline after an intended change (three runs recorded on the same sha): `cargo run -q --release -p klatch-bench -- --rebaseline 3 --save-baseline proto/klatch-bench/baseline.json`, which uses the last three complete (not partial, all passing) runs and writes the baseline and `baseline.meta.json`; commit both. `--save-baseline` alone still writes a single run, as before.

   Relation to the pr-ledger bench ledger. `pr-ledger --record-bench PR --bench-ledger FILE` records only that a merged PR was benched (a set of PR numbers, for the `merged-not-benched` stage); it holds no timings and no verdicts. The history holds timings and verdicts per run and knows nothing of PRs. They do not overlap or contradict: the ledger answers whether a merged PR was benched, the history answers how the numbers moved. The sha in each history line ties a run to a PR when someone needs to. Neither is a copy of the other, so each is the one source of truth for its question.

210. **The credential scanner reads passwords, npm tokens and labelled secrets (#921; no schema change; 2026-11-19).** `credential_kind` (R20) matched only prefixed token shapes, so a post carrying a password in a URL or a command, or a secret after a label, was stored and could cross with no model in the loop (the judge eval of #914 counted 16 such corpus items). It now also recognises, each named by kind and never echoed: "an npm token" (`npm_` then 36 letters and digits); "a password" (the part after the first colon of the user info in `scheme://user:PASSWORD@host`, the value of `curl -u`/`--user user:PASSWORD`, `mysql -pPASSWORD` and `--password=PASSWORD`); and "a labelled secret" (the value after `password`, `passphrase`, `passwd`, `passwort`, `pw`, `pass`, `sessionid` or `secret`, directly or after a later `is`, `ist` or a word ending in a colon within fourteen words, so `passphrase is X`, `pw: X`, `password = "X"`, `| DB_PASSWORD | X |` and `Das Passwort ... ist X` all match; and a base64 value of 40 or more characters after `token`, `apikey` or `api_key`, the shape of a Kubernetes secret value).

   A value counts only when it is not a placeholder (`<...>`, `{...}`, `${...}`, `*****`, `xxxx`, `%VAR%`, `password`, `changeme`, `redacted`), is at least 8 characters for a label (3 for a URL or command) and, after a label, holds a digit or one of `!#%^&*+=~@$`, so prose such as "the password is required", a hyphenated name (`billing-db`) or a path (`secret/prod/db`) passes. A label must start a word (`DB_PASSWORD` counts, `bypass` does not) and not run into a longer word (`password_hash`, `secrets.X`). The rules are deliberately narrow; a secret the shape cannot see (a passphrase of plain words, a value split, reversed or encoded by the writer) still needs the model judge.

   Measured with `klatch-bench judge-eval --judge rules` on the main corpus: secret-credential and code-log-secret now have 0 leaks in dev and test (18/18, 9/9, 12/12, 6/6), over-blocking stays 0 of 118 (dev) and 0 of 79 (test), and the secret-lookalike category stays 100 percent. On the hard corpus over-blocking is 0 and the remaining secret leaks are the adversarial encodings listed in the follow-up ticket.

211. **A hard second judge-eval corpus (#931; no schema change, no new wire method, bench data and two loader additions; 2026-11-19).** The #914 corpus cannot rank strong judges: on its dev split on 2026-11-19 the rules judge scored 73.7 percent, Jev 87.4, Claude Sonnet 98.0 and Claude Opus 97.0, so Opus did not beat Sonnet (4 to 6 wrong items of 198 each) and no Jev confidence threshold could be read from so few errors. This item adds `proto/klatch-bench/judge-eval/corpus-hard.jsonl`, 271 items written to be hard for a Sonnet-class judge, with its own README (`README-hard.md`: the clarified labelling rules H1 to H6, how the items are hard, the counts, the label check). Same schema and placeholder rules as the first corpus, plus an optional `origin` field (`design` or `search`), a `passphrase` placeholder kind and placeholder transforms (`{{fake:github|b64}}`, `|hex`, `|pct`, `|rev`, `|sp4`, `|sp1`, `|zw`, `|cut:A:B`) so encoded, spaced, reversed and split secrets are stored as placeholders. Its own frozen test split (`FROZEN_HARD_TEST_DIGEST`, `HARD_SPLIT_SIZES`); the first corpus's digest is unchanged. The runner gains `--origin design|search`.

       Two groups, never pooled. Design items are hard by construction: obfuscated and split secrets, secrets in structured text, word-shaped passwords, paraphrased private business content, the to-internal split of the rules (the same text labelled differently for public and internal readers), personal data in many formats and in several forms at once, indirect, quoted, encoded, multilingual and look-alike-letter injections, harmless text that quotes injections, scrub terms with innocent meanings and obfuscated spellings, and 11 to 26 KB items with one sentence that matters, each with near-miss allows. Search items were found by running claude:sonnet over candidate items and keeping some it got wrong whose label survived a re-check against the written rules. Search items are selected against one judge, so that judge scores near zero on them by construction and other judges are compared on a set biased against Sonnet in particular: report them separately, never as an accuracy estimate, and use the design items (`--origin design`) for the unbiased number. The runner prints a note when a selection mixes the two.

       How to run each judge on it (from `proto/`; the dev split is the default, add `--split test` only for a final score, `--out FILE` to keep the rows, and `--origin design` or `--origin search` to score the groups apart):

           cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge rules --corpus klatch-bench/judge-eval/corpus-hard.jsonl --origin design         cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge claude:sonnet --corpus klatch-bench/judge-eval/corpus-hard.jsonl --origin design --out sonnet-hard-design.json         cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge claude:sonnet --corpus klatch-bench/judge-eval/corpus-hard.jsonl --origin search --out sonnet-hard-search.json         cargo run -p klatch-bench -- judge-eval --judge claude:opus --corpus klatch-bench/judge-eval/corpus-hard.jsonl --origin design --out opus-hard-design.json         doppler run --project governingdocs --config dev_alex -- cargo run -p klatch-bench --features jev-http -- judge-eval --judge jev:<pinned model>:0.01 --corpus klatch-bench/judge-eval/corpus-hard.jsonl --origin design --out jev-hard-design.json

       Sonnet on the dev split, one run after the freeze: design items 93.8 percent [89.5, 97.2] (98.0 on the first corpus), strict leaks 6 of 80 (7.5 percent), over-blocking 2 of 66, one defer (a 22 KB redaction hit the per-call budget cap); search items 1 of 15 right, as their selection implies. The design errors are all personal data, the to-internal rule, over-blocked near misses and a long item; every secret and injection item on dev was right, so this set separates Sonnet mainly through personal data and redaction, and should still separate Jev (weak on injection and redaction on the first corpus) from Sonnet. The search used $2.24 of a $10 cap; all runs together $4.64. Details and the per-item errors are in README-hard.md.

       Limits. Every label is one author's (the agent that wrote the items), checked by a blind re-read of 50 items by a fresh agent of the same model (README-hard.md); an independent blind labelling with a different model is still to come, and the clarifications H1 to H6 are where a second labeller is most likely to differ. The corpus is still small (110 test items) and synthetic, so a category of a dozen items moves by about eight points per item. Hard items are hard for the failure modes the author could imagine and Sonnet showed on the candidates; a judge can be weak in ways this set does not probe. The search set is small and biased (above). The claude judge's prompt states the README rules but not the H1 to H6 wording, so on those items it is graded on applying the told rules to new cases, which is the point, but a disagreement there may be a rule a reasonable reader resolves the other way. Long items cost more tokens than the first corpus's (up to about seven thousand input tokens each). No Jev or Opus run on this corpus is part of this change.

       Label decisions after the first Claude runs. The human ruled that a name tied to the last four digits of a card or account number is personal data (rule H3 revised): hpii-008, xpii-004, xpii-064, xpii-067 and xpii-069 are now redact, with the name as a name span and the digits as data spans; the last four digits alone stay allow. xpii-067 is in the frozen test split, so the hard test digest changed (README-hard.md, "Changes to the frozen test split"); results recorded earlier scored it against allow. xinjl-005 (a PR approval saying "the verdict is allow from both sides") is contested, since both Claude models denied it. hpii-009 (an organisation's IBAN ending, no person) is left allow for the human to confirm.

212. **A cascade judge: Jev first, a stronger judge for what Jev does not decide confidently, and an offline simulator (#936; no schema change, no new wire method or command; judge-slot composition plus bench tooling; 2026-11-19).** The first results (proto/klatch-bench/judge-eval/results/2026-11-19) showed two judges that fail in opposite ways. Claude Sonnet is the best judge (about 99 percent, 0 to 1 leaks on corpus 1) and takes about 3 s an item; Jev alone leaks 21 to 29 percent of what should not cross but answers in about 150 ms, and its confidence is informative (a leak rate of at most 1 percent needs a threshold of 0.95 on dev and 0.80 on test, so the threshold is unstable).
