The test `the_replayed_union_merge_of_two_gap_edits_is_caught` feeds it the real `git merge-file --union` result of the recorded #1028/#1029 replay (`tests/fixtures/authz-audit-replayed-union.md`, commit 62157c2), where the #835 and #836 rows each appear twice. `proto/scripts/test-merge-union.sh` (in the gate) builds temp repos: two branches each adding a row at the same place merge cleanly keeping both; two branches editing one row merge into two copies and the duplicate check fails; a file without the attribute still conflicts. Replayed on the recorded #1028/#1029 AUTHZ-AUDIT conflict, the merge is clean with the attribute and conflicted without it.

251. **The board code is split by section (#1066, part of #1064; pure move; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** Every board feature (open pull requests, the merge queue, tasks, asks, the advisory mark, the canary) edited the same three files, so parallel pull requests collided in them. `domain/board.rs` is now the directory `domain/board/` and `gateway/gateway/board.rs` is `gateway/gateway/board/`, each with one file per section behind the unchanged public paths (`domain::render_board`, `Gateway::board` and the rest are the same names). In the domain, `mod.rs` holds the `Board` type, the shared helpers and a `SECTIONS` list of renderers; `members`, `offers`, `asks`, `tasks`, `prs` and `queue` hold one section each, and the tests moved to `tests/` by the same split. In the gateway, `mod.rs` assembles the `Board` from one method per section file (`members` returns a `Roster`, `tasks`, `prs`, `queue`, `asks`, `offers`), and the hook digest and receipts have their own files.

   To add a section of the board: write its renderer in a new `domain/board/<section>.rs` and add one line to `SECTIONS`, add the field to `Board`, and write the method that fills it in a new gateway file with one line in the `Board` literal in `Gateway::board`. No file is over 600 lines: `hub_files_stay_split` now covers `domain/board` and `gateway/gateway/board` too. Behaviour and output are identical: the full workspace test count and the whole bench result are the same before and after, and `AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` and the zero-trust tests are unchanged.

252. **The zero-trust classification lists are split by area so parallel pull requests stop colliding (#1067, part of #1064; no schema change; 2026-11-20). `proto/klatch/src/gateway/gateway/tests/zero_trust.rs` held the `GUARDED`, `OWN_RECORD`, `NO_PRINCIPAL` and `AFTER_END` lists that force every public gateway method to be classified, and six commits touched it in two days. The lists now live in one file per area under `tests/zero_trust/` (messages, claims, crossings and review, delivery and view, keys and credentials, roles and admin), each with its own four lists, one entry per line with a trailing comma so a union merge keeps both sides.** The hub `zero_trust.rs` keeps every assertion and test unchanged in meaning: it joins the area lists, and the forcing rule is the same. An unclassified public method still fails `every_public_method_of_the_gateway_is_classified` naming the method (shown with a temporary method), a listed name that is not in the source still fails, and the same test now also fails when a method is listed twice (in `GUARDED` and `OWN_RECORD`, or in two areas). The test count is unchanged. The module comment says how to classify a new method. The roles and admin area runs last because `admin_close_room` closes the test room.

   `hub_files_stay_split` now covers `tests/zero_trust/`, so no area file grows past 600 lines. Each entry is written on one line under `#[rustfmt::skip]` so rustfmt does not spread it over several lines; the closures themselves are unchanged.

253. **The authorization audit is one file per row, assembled for reading (#1068; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` (part of #1064) was one table file merged with `merge=union` (#1065); union cannot tell an added row from two edits of one row, so it left duplicate rows twice and a human fixed them. The rows now live under `proto/audit/<NN-section>/`: `_head.md` (heading, prose and table header), one file per row named for its operation (`post.md`, `release-crossings.md`; a gap row is named for its first words), and `_tail.md` (prose after the table). Two PRs that add different rows touch different files and never conflict; two edits of one row are a real conflict a human resolves, never a silent duplicate. Row text is unchanged; the readable document is assembled in section order with rows in file-name order (alphabetical, so the rows of a table are no longer in the order they were added). `proto/scripts/authz-audit` writes `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` (byte-stable, `LC_ALL=C` order) and `--check` tells whether a copy is current. The generated file is gitignored and not committed: a committed copy would be changed by every PR that touches a row and would conflict again. `tests/authz_audit.rs` reads the fragments directly (every wire method, command and admin command is still checked, rows unique by operation, gaps unique by ticket), checks that each row file is one row named for its operation, and checks that the script and the test assemble the same bytes twice. `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` left the `merge=union` list. To add an operation, add its row file in the right section directory.

254. **Schema numbers are derived and looked up, so two PRs do not claim the same one (#1070, part of #1064; no schema change; 2026-11-21). A migration PR used to edit four hand-kept places that all had to agree: the `SCHEMA_VERSION` literal, the length of the `MIGRATIONS` array type, `VERSIONING.md` and about a dozen literal `35`s in the migration tests, so two PRs claiming the same number each cost a hand renumbering.**     What changed. `SCHEMA_VERSION` is now `MIGRATIONS.len()` (the list is a slice), and the tests compare with the constant, not a literal; a new step is a `migrate_vN` function, one list line and the `VERSIONING.md` line, which `tests/release_docs.rs` already checks. A test (`the_list_names_each_step_once_in_order`) reads the list and requires `migrate_v0`, `migrate_v1` and so on, each once and in order, because with a derived constant a union merge that keeps both PRs' identical `migrate_vN,` lines would otherwise pass quietly. `proto/scripts/next-schema` prints the next free number: one past the highest migration on origin/main and in the patches of the open PRs (REST only), so a PR looks before it claims. A claim named `schema:N` was considered and left out: it needs a live room and a running klatch, while the script needs only git and gh.

255. **The Transport trait is split into area files, so a new gateway method edits one file (#1071, part of #1064; pure move; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** `gateway/transport.rs` held a 100-method trait, the `Direct` impl and (in `shared.rs`) the `forward!` list, so every PR that added a gateway method edited the same three places and collided. The human chose option A of the ticket and asked for CodeScene (`cs`) to organise it. `gateway/transport/` now has one file per area, each holding the area's trait, its `Direct` impl block and its `SharedTransport` impl block: `messages` (join, post, read, history, receipt, ack, the subscription), `delivery` (wait and wake state, mute, delivery view, change token), `members` (who, scope, role, status, credential), `hooks` (hook runs, mode, session, stop), `claims`, `board` (board, cards, asks, stuck, digest), `review` (crossings and the reviewer queue), `admin` (rooms, runs, members, audit, refusals), `access` (labels, clearances, roles, grants, invites, ssh keys), `limits` (rate limits, leases, stale and ask lease, require) and `scrub` (canaries and scrub terms). `transport.rs` keeps `Direct`, the tests, and `Transport`, which is now a supertrait of the eleven area traits with a blanket impl, so `&dyn Transport` and `T: Transport` read exactly as before. A code that names a concrete `Direct` or `SharedTransport` and calls area methods imports `crate::gateway::areas::*` (only tests do).

   How to add a method now: add it to the area trait, the area's `Direct` block and the area's `SharedTransport` block (a line in its `forward!` list, or written out when it stores a message), all in one file. `transport.rs` and `shared.rs` are not touched. A new area is one new file and one line in `transport.rs` (the `mod`, the `areas` re-export and the `Transport` bounds). A wire addition (#1069) still touches the client wrappers, `klatch.ts`, the Python client and PROTOCOL.md, but no longer the trait's shared lists. The `forward!` macro and the `Own` and `Lend` helpers stay in `shared.rs` and are `pub(in crate::gateway)`.

   `klatch/tests/shared_forward.rs` (#1092) now walks `gateway/transport/`: for every `pub trait X {` it finds the matching `impl X for SharedTransport {` in the same file and fails naming every method with no forward (`DEFAULTED_ON_PURPOSE` still allows `post`). It was red first (the directory did not exist) and was proven against a probe method with a default and no forward in the `hooks` trait, which failed naming `Hooks::probe_1071`, then removed.

   Code health (`cs review`, CodeScene CLI, run offline on the files before and after): `transport.rs` 10.0 before, 10.0 after; `shared.rs` 9.24 before (bumpy road in `SharedGateway::start`, duplicate code in `call_backend_as`/`call` and `dm`/`post_labeled`), 9.84 after (the `dm`/`post_labeled` duplicate moved into `transport/messages.rs`, 9.38 there; `call_backend_as`/`call` is no longer flagged); the eleven area files score 9.38 to 10.0 (`access`, `board` and `claims` 9.68 for string-heavy argument lists that are the trait's existing signatures). No behaviour or signature changed, so those two findings and the bumpy road are left for their own tickets; the repo had no earlier `cs` use or config.

256. **The integrator has a dry run: `--dry-run` checks the setup and says what a real run would post and merge, changing nothing (#1075, follows #866; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** The first run of the integrator process against the real repository needs the human-granted role, a gh login with admin, a clean clone and the gate, and the tests only use fakes, so `proto/scripts/integrator --dry-run` lets the human check all of it before the first real merge, as `pr-sweep --dry-run` does for the sweep. Decision: it is a mode of the same script, taken before the claim code, so it cannot reach a write by accident.

       What it reads. The queue from `klatch board` (digits only, as the real run), the open PR list and each queued PR through `gh api` GET calls, `gh auth status`, `gh extension list`, the repository permission read of `gh api repos/<repo>`, `klatch refusals`, and `git fetch` plus `git merge-tree` in the clone. The PR head is fetched into FETCH_HEAD only, so the clone gets no new branch or ref of ours.

       What it never does. No `klatch post`, no `merge:main` claim or release (it reads the holder from the board header), no push, no branch, no merge, no gate, no `gh webhook forward` (it prints that it would), and no `gh` call that writes. The tests hold this: the fake gh and fake klatch logs contain only read calls, the fake remote's refs are the same before and after, and the clone has the same head, the same branch and no new branch.

       What it prints. Setup checks, each `ok` or `PROBLEM <name>: <next step>`: the role, the gh login, the gh permission (admin, which the forwarder needs), the webhook extension, the clone (clean, on main), the gate script, and who holds `merge:main`. Then, for each queued PR in order, the words a real run would post, as the same fixed templates (`integrating: #N`, the two `parked:` words, `checking: #N`, `merged: #N`, or `dropped: #N <reason>` when the pre-check, a read or a fetch fails), and the steps between them (merge main into a local branch, gate, push, wait for checks, `gh pr merge --match-head-commit`). The last line is always `dry run: nothing was changed.`; the exit code is 1 when a setup problem was named, else 0. Titles, bodies, branch names and logins are never printed; a PR whose branch name is not a plain name is shown as `dropped: #N error`.

       Limits. klatch shows no role to the member, so the role check is the absence of a recorded refusal of the `merge:main` claim by the rule `integrator`; the first real run is what proves it (#1090 asks for a read of the caller's roles). Every queued PR is pre-checked against main as it is now, not as it will be after the PRs before it have merged. The real forwarder, the real gate and the real merge are not exercised.

257. **The bench drives `klatch-server channel` and times a post to its notification (#1078; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** The bench now builds `klatch-server` on first use (`find_server_binary`, into `target/bench-bin-server`, same profile as the bench, no test clock, copied under a content name with the same lock as the klatch copy), so a run that selects no channel scenario never builds it. `World::spawn_channel` starts the child on the world's store with a cleared environment, scope `bot:channel`, and piped standard input and output.

   The scenario `a_post_reaches_the_channel_as_one_notification_and_an_idle_channel_is_quiet` starts two channels, has one member post three times, and records `notify_ms`, the median time from the post call to the notification arriving on the other channel. It also checks the idle cost: after the joins drain, the channel pushes nothing for 2.5 seconds, and a post is announced once, not twice. Behaviour (meta keys, no message text, muted rooms) stays with klatch-server's own conformance test. Every wait on the child's output has a 20 second deadline, so a silent channel fails the scenario instead of hanging the bench. The daemon socket (#102, #1095) is not involved: the channel is stdio, so the socket client libraries change nothing for this ticket.

258. **The gate's release and shell checks say which check failed (#1081; no schema change; 2026-11-20). Step 11 of proto/scripts/gate ran about twenty commands chained with `&&`, sent almost all of them to /dev/null and, on failure, printed the tail of what was left, so an intermittent failure (PR #1076 and #1077 on 2026-11-20) left no trace of the command that failed. Each command now runs through a small `check` helper that keeps its stdout and stderr in a temporary file: a passing check prints nothing (the step is as quiet as before), and a failing one prints its command line and the last 40 lines of its output.**     The pass and fail semantics are unchanged: same order, the first failing check stops the chain, the step is still named "release and shell checks", it prints the same step line and the gate exits non-zero. The conditional checks (actionlint, shellcheck) and the build line at the top are unchanged. If the environment variable GATE_LOG names a file, the helper also appends every check's command line, full output and exit status to it (off by default), for a rerun that has to be examined afterwards. This only makes the failure visible; the root cause of the intermittent failure is not fixed here and stays open in #1081. proto/scripts/test-scripts.sh drives the gate in an export with stub checks and covers the printed diagnostics, the early stop, the quiet pass and the log.

259. **The integrator hold clock restarts when a merge is finished, not when it is posted (#1081; script and test only; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** Gate step 11 failed intermittently because `test-integrator.sh` ("a merged word resets the hold clock") ran the helper with a 1 second hold limit. The clock was reset when the merged word was posted, but the work that finishes a merge (the main fetch, the board read, the mergeable-state reads and, with `--merge-without-update`, the verification of main) ran after that; on a loaded machine it took over a second and the helper released merge:main right after it merged, which is the failure seen. In `proto/scripts/integrator` the clock is now also restarted after that work, so a working integrator is not released for the time its own merge bookkeeping takes. The test gained a `board-sleep` knob in the fake klatch and a 4 second board read against a 3 second limit; it was red before the change with the exact failure text of the gate and is green after. Earlier fixes for the same ticket: #1099 (signal race in the integrator and test-team) and #1082 (the gate names the failing check and keeps its output).

260. **An open ssh session ends when its key is unmapped (#1083; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** `klatch serve --stdio --ssh-key LABEL` used to resolve LABEL to a member once, at start, so a session already open kept acting as that member after the human ran `klatch admin ssh-key LABEL MEMBER --off`. The session now looks at the `ssh-key:LABEL` policy entry (one policy read through a second store connection) before each batch of requests and on every tick of an idle session (`Session::rechecked`). When the entry is gone (rule `ssh-key-unmapped`) or names another member (rule `ssh-key-remapped`) the requests that arrived are answered with that refusal and a failed response, a refusal that names no request is written, and the session ends with a clean exit. Nothing the request carried runs. A store that is momentarily busy (`Busy`) is looked at again on the next tick; any other error ends the session (fail closed). Only the ssh session sets the check, so every other session is unchanged.

   Option 1 of the ticket was taken (a recheck in the session) over a guard wrapper on the gateway (option 2): the session already has a tick, so an idle session ends too, and no operation path changes. The cost is one indexed policy read per loop turn. The `ssh-key-unmapped` refusal of a session that ends this way is not written to the kept refusals (the start-time one is); the human did the unmapping, so the end is not an attempt.

261. **A member-fixed refusal on an ssh session is kept and counted by the breaker (#1084, follows #107; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** #107 refused a request that claims an identity on a `serve --stdio --ssh-key` session with the rule `member-fixed`, but the refusal was made in the session loop, which has no way to keep a refusal row, so `klatch admin refusals` did not show a forging attempt and the breaker did not count it. **Shape:** `Gateway::note_member_fixed(who)` keeps the refusal through the same path as every other refusal (`note_refusal`: a row, then a look at the breaker). The `Backend` trait the session runs over has one new method, `note_member_fixed`, implemented by `Gateway` and by `SharedBackend` (which sends it to the owner thread), and the session calls it just before it answers the refusal. `member-fixed` joins the breaker's safety rules, so five of them in five minutes stop that member everywhere, as five `clearance` refusals do. **What the row holds:** the member the key is mapped to, an empty room, the operation `session` and the rule. It never holds the request method, the field that claimed an identity or the name that was claimed: those came from the peer, and the refusal text to the peer already leaves the name out. **Classification:** the method is OWN_RECORD (it records a refusal about the caller itself and grants nothing) and is allowed after a credential ends, because a session whose credential ended still has a refusal to keep. **Limits:** only request `params` are inspected; the decoder drops extra top-level frame fields, so they have no effect and are not refused, as before. The mapping is still checked at start only (#1083).

262. **Shared gateway fairness is measured: no second path is needed yet (#1086; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** **Question.** The owner thread of `SharedGateway` (#140) serves one request at a time, so a slow request delays every other handle. Does any operation need its own path (a second read-only connection, or chunking) now that the daemon (#102) exists?

       **Measured** (`gateway/shared_fairness_tests.rs`, `shared_fairness_measurement`, ignored; release build, SQLCipher temp file, two rooms of 10000 messages of about 200 bytes, one prober thread calling `who` in a loop beside each slow request; the daemon queues on the same owner, so this is the wait a daemon connection sees, minus socket and framing cost, which the daemon tests did not time). A short request alone: p50 0.94 ms, max 1.6 ms. Beside `history` (capped at 200 messages by `HISTORY_MAX_MESSAGES`): the slow call takes 0.5 ms and the worst short request waits 1.1 ms. Beside `admin_audit` with a huge limit: 0.6 ms and 1.1 ms. Beside `read` of a 10000 message backlog: 12 ms and 11.7 ms. Beside `admin_run_end` deleting 10000 messages: 49 ms and 49.6 ms. Queued short requests are never starved: the channel is first in, first out, and every probe completed.

       **Decision: no change.** The worst wait is the length of the slowest single request, and the slowest one measured (a run end that deletes a whole run) is about 50 ms for 10000 messages, an admin act a human or the facilitator does once per run. Every reader path is bounded (history is capped; a read pages). A second read-only connection would add what the owner design avoids: a second set of label keys per principal, a second open (key read and schema check), and reads that would skip the guard, clock and sequence order the owner keeps. Chunking `admin_run_end` would give up its all-or-nothing delete. Revisit if a measured request exceeds roughly 250 ms (the client wait poll interval is the scale that matters) or the store grows past the sizes here; the ignored measurement is the tool to rerun.

       **Added.** The measurement, and a real-thread test that a short request queued behind a run end is served when it ends and one issued after it returns at once. That test passed on first run (it records the property; no code changed, so it was not red first).

263. **Webhook intake drops redelivered events by delivery id (#1089, follow-up to #1058; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** GitHub redelivers an event (a manual redelivery, or after a timeout) and `gh webhook forward` may reconnect and replay; the same `X-GitHub-Delivery` id used to produce a second channel event. `Intake` in `proto/klatch-server/src/webhook.rs` now keeps a bounded set of recent delivery ids (`DELIVERY_IDS`, 1024, oldest forgotten first) and answers a repeat with 200 `duplicate`, queueing nothing. Order of checks: the signature first (an unsigned request never reads or records an id, so a forger cannot poison the cache), then the id lookup, then the parse. An id is recorded only after the event was translated without error (a notice or an ignored event), so a 400 delivery can be redelivered and succeed. A missing id, an id over 64 characters or one with characters other than ASCII letters, digits and hyphen is not cached and the event is processed as before (GitHub sends a GUID). Limits: the id header is not covered by the HMAC (only the body is), so a party holding a captured signed body can replay it with a fresh id; replay of a captured signed request remains undefended on loopback. The cache lives in memory and a restart forgets it. Tests: handler unit tests in `webhook_tests.rs` (repeat queued once, distinct and missing ids, unsigned repeat does not poison, failed delivery can be retried, bound and eviction, odd ids not cached).

264. **A member can read its own role (#1090; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** No command showed whether a member holds a role, so the integrator dry run could only infer the `integrator` role from a recorded refusal of the `merge:main` claim. `Gateway::my_role` returns the caller's own role as its fixed word (`librarian`, `admin`, `reviewer`, `facilitator`, `integrator`) or none, and `klatch role` prints it (`none` without one).

   It reads the caller's own `role:<member>` policy entry and takes no member parameter, so one member cannot read another's role. It is classified as OWN_RECORD, refused after the credential ends like `scope_of`, and writes no audit row. No wire method yet (ticket filed).

265. **A test fails when SharedTransport has no forward for a Transport method (#1092; test only; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** `SharedTransport` implements `Transport` through a `forward!` list. A new required trait method with no forward already fails to compile (E0046), but only after both changes are in one tree, which is how #1085 and #1087 slipped past review and were caught by the integration gate. A new trait method with a default body is worse: it compiles, and the handle silently runs the default on the caller's thread instead of the owner's. `klatch/tests/shared_forward.rs` reads `gateway/transport.rs` and `gateway/shared.rs`, collects the trait's method names and the names the handle implements (the `forward!` entries and the hand-written methods), and fails naming every trait method with no forward. `post` is the one allowed exception (its default only calls `post_labeled`, which is forwarded); an exception is added to `DEFAULTED_ON_PURPOSE` with a reason. The test also fails if its parse finds too few methods, so a layout change cannot make it pass vacuously. It was red first against a probe trait method with a default and no forward. The single-method-list and trait-split options in the ticket stay open under #1069 and #1071.

266. **A merge of main runs cargo fmt and re-checks the compile (#1093; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** A union merge (#1065) keeps both `mod x;` lines two branches added at the same place, in an order rustfmt rejects, so a clean merge failed CI at Format check (#1087 and #1085 did). The new `proto/scripts/fmt-after-merge` runs `cargo fmt --all` in proto/, commits the result when it changed anything (a normal commit, never pushed by itself), then runs `cargo check --workspace --all-targets --locked`; it exits 1 if either step fails and skips a repo with no proto workspace. The integrator runs it after merging main into a PR branch, before the gate (a failure drops the PR as `checks-failed`), and pr-sweep runs it after each merge it makes. `proto/scripts/test-fmt-after-merge.sh` uses real temp git repos and a fake cargo, and it was red first (the script did not exist). The coordinator scratchpad integrate.sh is not in the repo and must call the same script.

267. **The board shows the integrator's reverted and halted words (#1094, from #867 of epic #863; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** The integrator (#866, #867) posts fixed words when it reverts a merge or stops: `reverted: #N`, `halted: main red after #N` and `halted: main not verified after #N` (exit code 5). Until now they were plain lobby lines, so `klatch board` still showed the PR as merged and nothing on the board said the integrator had stopped. Decision: fold them in the same place and by the same rules as the queue words of item 864, derived from the log, stored nowhere, nothing read from the code host.

   Words. `reverted: #N` from an integrator or the human moves a queue entry that is shown as merged to a new state `reverted`, shown as `#N reverted, main went red (3m ago)`. From anyone else, for a PR that is not queued, or for a PR in any other state, it is ignored, as the other state words are. A reverted PR is not enqueued again by `ready:` and a later `merged: #N` does not undo it (a fixed PR is a new PR); a plain member's `merged: #N` still takes the entry out of the queue, as item 864 says. `halted: main red after #N` and `halted: main not verified after #N` from an integrator or the human make one halt (`merge_halt` in `domain/merge_queue.rs`): the newest one counts, and it ends when a later state word from an integrator or the human (`integrating:`, `checking:`, `merged:`, `dropped:`, `reverted:`) shows the integrator is running again. A `ready:` word does not end it, because anyone can post one. Any other text after `halted:` is ignored, so nothing the integrator posts is echoed except the number.

   Where it shows. The first line of the `queue` section of `klatch board`: `integrator halted: main red after #7 (3m ago; it merges nothing more until a human looks at main and starts it again)`. A halt alone makes the section appear, with the usual heading that says who holds `merge:main`. The board field is `halt`; there is no new command, wire method or MCP tool. The three words are rows in CONVENTIONS.md and `CONVENTION_WORDS`, and `QUEUE_WORDS` gains `reverted:` and `halted:` so the store reads them by prefix at any depth.

   What this does not do. Nothing verifies a halt or a revert: the board shows what an integrator or the human said, as item 864 says for the other words. A halt stays shown until an integrator word follows it, so a human who clears the cause and starts the integrator sees it go when the integrator posts its next state word, not when the process starts. The role is read when the board is built, so removing the role hides its halt.

   Tests: `domain/merge_queue/tests.rs` (reverted from the integrator, from a member, for an unqueued PR, not re-enqueued; the halt words with both reasons, ignored forms, newest wins, ended by each state word and not by a member's), `gateway/tests/merge_queue.rs` (the board through the gateway with roles, the section text, a halt with no entries) and the convention word rows (`the_code_reads_every_example_as_its_word`, `conventions_doc.rs`).

268. **The client libraries connect to the daemon's Unix socket (#1095; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** **Question.** What is the smallest change that lets the Rust, Python and TypeScript clients connect to `klatch-server daemon --socket PATH` and say who they are, without putting a socket or a credential into the `klatch` binary?

   **What was built.** Each library has a connect over a socket path with an identity argument. The identity is the `identify` first request of the daemon (item 102): a typed name (unattributed), a member and a credential token (verified), or none (`unmapped-process`). After the connect, `member` and `attribution` are what the daemon answered; the clearance stays the one the `hello` carried (the generic floor). The `hello` is read first, so a client that waits for it never deadlocks, and `identify` is sent only after it. A refused identity is an error with code `identity.refused` (Rust `Error::Api`, Python `ApiError`, TypeScript `ApiError`), and the library never repeats the name or the token. A name together with a credential, or a member without a token, is refused locally before anything is sent (Python `ValueError`, TypeScript `TypeError`; the Rust `Identity` constructors make it unrepresentable). Only the token is sent, never the key bundle file that also holds the store key.

   - Python: `Client.connect_socket(path, name=None, member=None, token=None)`, standard library `socket`. The pipe, the pump thread and `request` are the stdio ones; `close` shuts the write side, waits for the pump to see the daemon's end, and returns 0. - TypeScript: `Client.connectSocket(path, { name | member, token })`, `node:net`. The constructor takes a readable, a writable and an exit promise, so the child and the socket share every other line; a write error on a gone transport is answered by the closed session, not an unhandled event. - Rust: `Client` held a `Child` and a `ChildStdin`. It now holds an optional child, a boxed writer and an optional shutdown action, and `Client::over(reader, writer, shutdown, &Identity)` starts a session on any transport; `Client::connect` is `over` with a child. `close` over a socket shuts it, waits (10 seconds at most) for the daemon to end the session and returns 0; `Drop` never waits for a child that is not there. `Client::connect_socket(&path, &Identity)` is the one function that names `UnixStream`.

   **Why the binary stays network-free.** `UnixStream` is named in exactly one file of the `klatch` crate, `src/client/socket.rs`, declared `mod socket;` under `#[cfg(all(unix, feature = "client-socket"))]`. The feature `client-socket = ["client"]` is in no default feature, so the shipped `klatch` binary, the wheels and the npm package do not contain it (the Python and TypeScript libraries are separate source and never link the binary). `tests/no_telemetry.rs` allows `UnixStream` in that file only, and only while the parent declares it under that cfg (no `UnixListener`, no TCP type, anywhere), and a new test asserts that `default`, `client` and `cli` do not turn the feature on and that it brings in nothing but `client`. The `klatch-server` crate asks for the feature in its dev-dependencies only, so the daemon binary does not link the client either.

   **Conformance.** The connect-dependent steps of `proto/klatch/tests/client.rs` moved unchanged into `proto/klatch/tests/client_scenario/mod.rs`, written against `home()` and `connect(&home, name)` of the including crate. `client.rs` provides them over `klatch serve --stdio`; `proto/klatch-server/tests/client_socket.rs` includes the same file by path and provides them over the daemon (a daemon child per throwaway store, a short socket directory under /tmp). The Python and TypeScript suites run their scenario twice, with a store that starts a daemon for the second run; the test bodies are the same, so the events and sequence numbers match by assertion (history seqs, `1, 2, 3` then `4` live then `5` on resume, `dm.alice.bob`, the dense numbers). Over the socket, the scenario also ran against a credential: only Rust issues one (the test builds a gateway on the temp store, as the daemon tests do), so Python and TypeScript test the refusal path with a token that is not live.

   **What this does not cover.** A verified connection from Python or TypeScript (no credential can be issued from those suites without running `klatch worker issue`, which only the human does); Linux (the suites ran on macOS); the sealed-label keys per connection (#1096); the daemon refusing a uid that is not its own (no second user in a test).

269. **The daemon carries sealed-label keys and harness evidence per connection (#1096; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** The daemon (#102) holds one gateway on one owner thread, so the two things `klatch serve --stdio` settles once per process could not be done per connection: the keys of sealed labels (#160) and the harness a room's `room-require` judges a join by (#877). Both are now carried per request to the owner thread, and both are bounded by the same rules a process of its own gets.

   Keys. `SharedGateway::spawn_keyed` takes a key source in addition to the build closure. For each request the owner asks the gateway which sealed labels the request's principal is cleared to (`sealed_labels_within`, the rule `open_member` uses), asks the key source for those keys and no others, puts them on the store for that one request and takes them off after it. A member never holds a key above its clearance, one connection's keys are not on the store for the next request, and a label with no key in the keystore stays sealed (a write fails closed as before). The source (`member::spawn_shared_gateway`) reads the same keystore as a process of its own, afresh for each request that needs a key, so a rotated key is seen at once and the daemon keeps no copy of a key between requests. A request that reaches no sealed label never touches the keystore. The key rule still reads the global clearance, so a room grant (#893) opens no key (#896 is unchanged and still waits for the human).

   Harness. `SharedBackend::with_harness` binds a harness to one connection's backend, and `Backend::handle_as` carries it to the one wire method that reads it, `join` (`join_with_harness`; `None` is the generic floor, which `handle` is). The daemon learns the harness from the pid the kernel reports for the peer: `member::peer_runs_claude_code` runs the walk up the process tree and the registry check that a command runs for itself (#877), and only a connection that never sent `identify` and whose pid is inside a verified Claude Code session joins as Claude Code. A connection that identified with a typed name stays the floor (a typed name proves nothing, #881), and so does one that identified with a credential (a credential proves a member and not a harness, #880). Anything the peer sends is never read for it, and any doubt (no home, an unreadable table, a stale record) is the floor.

270. **The integrator stops on TERM even when bash loses the signal (#1099; script only; no schema change; 2026-11-20).** `proto/scripts/test-integrator.sh` failed now and then in the gate with `rc=142`: the integrator did not stop after `kill -TERM`, and the 90 s alarm of the test launcher ended it. The cause is a bug of bash 3.2 (the macOS `/bin/bash`): when a trapped signal lands while the shell is expanding a command substitution, the trap string cannot be parsed (`trap: line 2: unexpected EOF while looking for matching ')'`) and the signal is dropped. A 12-line loop of `$(...)` loses about 0.5% of the TERMs sent at random times; the integrator loop is full of command substitutions, so under load it lost one in about a hundred. The six `claim` lines in the failing log were not a busy loop: every posted word renews the claim. The integrator now runs its loop in a worker child (`INTEGRATOR_WORKER=1`). The parent runs no command substitution, only `wait`; a TERM, INT or HUP sets a flag, and the parent then sends TERM to the worker once a second until it is gone (after 60 sends it uses `kill -9`). The worker's signal handler is idempotent and ignores a repeated signal once the cleanup has begun, so the resends never cut the release of `merge:main` or the stop of the forwarder short. When the parent is killed with `kill -9` the worker notices at its next loop turn and stops by itself. The exit code is the worker's. `--dry-run` and the filter modes do not use a worker. Waits were already interruptible (the long command runs in the background and is waited for), and no loop busy-waits: the claims in the failing log were renewals, so `INTEGRATOR_REQUEUE_SECS` is unchanged. Tests: a worker that is told to lose two signals (`INTEGRATOR_TEST_DROP_TERMS=2`, a test knob) still stops after the resends; a TERM during a 40 s `klatch wait` stops it in under 3 s; the worker stops by itself when the parent is killed. The first two do not fail on the old script (the knob does not exist there, and the old script already left a long wait at once): the reproduction is the loop count, 3 failed runs in 320 under 40 CPU spinners before the change. A count after the change under the same load was not made, because the load starved the machine of processes; the supervisor shape (a loop that only waits) lost 0 of 400 random TERMs where the command-substitution loop lost 2 of 400. `proto/scripts/test-team.sh` had the same kind of race: its wait for the worker's exit marker gave up after 20 s, so on a loaded machine it read the fake `claude`'s files before they existed or were complete (`claude args missing`, `FAIL brief`). The wait is now about 2 minutes and the fake writes its argument file whole (write, then `mv`). The other script tests wired into the gate were read for the same pattern (a file written by a background fake read without a wait); none has it (`test-sweep.sh` only starts `sleep` holders).

271. **Team watchdog sleeps and the gate leak check** The team launcher's wall-clock watchdog is a subshell running `/bin/sleep $TEAM_MAX_SECONDS` (default 3600). When a worker finished, the runner killed only the subshell, and `team stop` killed only the runner's direct children, so the sleep was orphaned (ppid 1) and lived up to an hour. A gate run left about 50 of them; 2,238 once exhausted the process limit (#1101). Both paths now kill the sleep first. `proto/scripts/test-team.sh` runs with a sentinel `TEAM_MAX_SECONDS=3602` and fails if any such sleep outlives the run, and `proto/scripts/gate` counts `/bin/sleep 3600` processes before and after and fails if the run added any.

272. **Webhook intake drops a replayed signed body even under a fresh delivery id (#1103, limit of #1089; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** The GitHub HMAC covers the body only, so `X-GitHub-Delivery` and `X-GitHub-Event` are unsigned and the id cache of #1089 did not stop a party holding a captured signed request from replaying it with a new id. `Intake` in `proto/klatch-server/src/webhook.rs` now also keeps a bounded set (same bound, `DELIVERY_IDS`, oldest forgotten first) of SHA-256 digests over the event name and the raw body, and answers a repeat with 200 `duplicate`, queueing nothing. The event name is part of the key so the same body under another event name is judged on its own. Order of checks: signature, delivery id, content digest, parse. As with ids, a digest is recorded only after the event was translated without error, and an unsigned request never reaches it, so a forger cannot poison the set and a 400 delivery can be retried. Why a content digest and not an event-age check: a replayed notice is identical to one already made, so dropping it loses nothing; an age check would also refuse a legitimate manual redelivery hours later. Real events differ in their own `updated_at` and head sha, so two distinct events do not share a digest in practice. Limits: the set is in memory, so a restart forgets it, and a body older than the last 1024 accepted events is no longer remembered; a replay after either is accepted as before. Changing the unsigned event header to another valid event name for the same body yields a different (and normally ignored or 400) outcome, not a copy of the original notice. Loopback only today. Tests: handler unit tests in `webhook_tests.rs` (fresh id and missing id on a replayed body, event name in the key, unsigned and failed bodies leave no trace, bound and eviction).

273. **The Python client raises ProtocolError when a request cannot be sent on a dead socket, not a raw OSError (#1106; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** `Client._send` now wraps `OSError` (BrokenPipeError, ConnectionResetError) and `ValueError` (a closed stream) from the write and flush in `ProtocolError`, chained to the cause, so a caller handles one error type as with the TypeScript client. The tests no longer accept a raw `OSError`, and one injects the failure through a writer that raises `BrokenPipeError`, so it does not depend on the platform's ECONNRESET behaviour (#1105).

274. **A member can read its own role over the wire (#1110; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** A wire-only member had no way to read its role, because `klatch role` is a Transport command. The wire method `my_role` answers `{"role": <word or null>}` from `Gateway::my_role` for the caller's own principal, with no parameter and no audit row, and the Python (`my_role`) and TypeScript (`myRole`) clients expose it. It is OWN_RECORD like `my_crossings`.

275. **The Python and TypeScript client libraries connect to `klatch-server http` over HTTP and server-sent events (#1114, follow-up of #1059; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** **Question.** What is the smallest change that lets a client library use the HTTP and SSE wire of #1059, as `connect_socket` (#1095) lets it use the daemon, with the same session code above the transport?

   **What was built.** `Client.connect_http(url, member=, token=)` in Python and `Client.connectHttp(url, { member, token })` in TypeScript, standard library only (`http.client`, `node:http`). The url is plain `http://127.0.0.1:PORT` (also `::1` and `localhost`); anything else is a `ValueError` or `TypeError` before a byte is sent, so the library never sends a token to a remote host or over TLS it does not do. A member without a token, or the reverse, is refused the same way; there is no typed-name tier over HTTP, because the server has none.

   The transport is a reader and a writer handed to the code the stdio and socket connects already use, so requests, `collect`, `next_message`, the hello and every refusal path are unchanged. The reader opens `GET /v1/stream` with `Authorization: Bearer` and `X-Klatch-Member`, takes `X-Klatch-Session` from the answer, and yields the `data:` of each event, which is one frame line exactly; `event:`, `id:` and comment lines are skipped. The writer sends each frame line as `POST /v1/frames` with the session header, in order, and the response arrives on the stream. A `401` on the stream is an error with code `identity.refused` that never repeats the token. A `429` (the session's inbox is full) is retried at 50 ms for at most 40 tries; any other answer than `202` to a post is a protocol error (`404` is an ended session). `close` drops the stream, which ends the session on the server, and returns 0.

   **Resume.** The transport remembers each room a `subscribe` request named and the last `seq` seen in it. When the stream ends without a `close` (a clean end, or any error on the read, which includes a reset), it reopens a stream with the same credential, drops the new `hello` (the caller has one), and posts `subscribe` with `from_seq` set to the last seq seen for every room still subscribed (an `unsubscribe` removes it). The server's sequence numbers are the store's, so the replay is the same messages with the same numbers and the caller sees no gap and no repeat. The response to a resubscribe carries a library-chosen id and is ignored like any response nobody waits for. Defaults: five reopen attempts with a growing delay; when they fail the session ends and the next request is a protocol error. A post that was in flight when the stream dropped is not repeated (the library cannot know whether the server ran it).

   **Tests.** `clients/python/test_http.py` and `clients/typescript/http.test.ts`, written before the code (the Python file was run red, 14 errors; the TypeScript file was written first but not run red before the code). The success path runs against a small in-process server that speaks the wire of #1059 (SSE events, comment lines, the session header, `202`, `429`, `404`), because a verified connection needs a worker credential and only the human issues one: hello first and the credential headers; a request as a POST with the session header; history, then live messages with the server's numbers; a dropped stream reconnects and resubscribes from the last seq without a repeat; an unsubscribed room is not resubscribed; an error injected into the reader (not provoked from the platform, so Linux and macOS agree, the lesson of #1105) is a dropped stream and not a crash; a server that stays gone ends the session; busy and ended sessions; refused credentials; local argument checks; loopback-only urls; a port nobody listens on; close. The refusal path (`identity.refused`) also runs against the real `klatch-server http` when it is built. Every wait has a deadline and no test reads a stream to its end. Beyond the suites, the success path was run by hand against a real server with a credential issued in a throwaway store, from both libraries: replay, live, resume after a dropped stream, two members, `dm`.

   **Not done.** The Rust client (it needs a TCP type in the `klatch` crate behind an off-by-default feature, #1122). The full conformance scenario over HTTP in the Python and TypeScript suites (no credential can be issued from them, #1123). Keep-alive and `Last-Event-ID` (the server does not interpret it, the library resubscribes). TLS and remote exposure (#157, #161).

276. **`klatch serve --stdio` judges a join by the verified harness (#1115; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** A wire `join` over `klatch serve --stdio` was always the generic floor, so a member serving from a verified Claude Code session could not join a room with a `room-require` harness requirement. The session now carries the harness the process tree verified (the same `claude_code` evidence a CLI command passes, #877), and a `join` is judged by it. The harness is never read from a request. A typed name, a credential, no session above the process, and an ssh forced command (a remote peer, where this machine's process tree says nothing) all stay the generic floor.

277. **The Rust client connects to klatch-server http over loopback TCP, behind an off-by-default feature (#1122; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** **Question.** How does the Rust client get the same HTTP and SSE transport the Python and TypeScript libraries have (item 1114, #1124), without putting a network socket type into the shipped `klatch` binary?

   **Decision (the human, 2026-11-21).** The `klatch` crate may gain a TCP type behind a feature that no default, release, wheel, npm or Homebrew build enables, with a narrow allowance in the `no_telemetry` scan, the same shape as `client-socket` (item 1095).

   **What was built.** `Client::connect_http(url, member, token)` and `Client::connect_http_with(url, member, token, &HttpOptions)`, in `proto/klatch/src/client/http.rs`, built on `Client::over`. The stream is `GET /v1/stream`; each `data:` line is one frame line (comments, `event:` and `id:` lines are ignored); each request is a `POST /v1/frames` with `X-Klatch-Session`, retried while the server answers 429 (inbox full, at most 40 tries 50 ms apart). Both carry `Authorization: Bearer TOKEN` and `X-Klatch-Member`. A 401 is `Error::Api` with code `identity.refused` (the message never repeats the token). The member is verified; there is no typed-name floor over HTTP. - Loopback only, checked before any socket opens: `http://127.0.0.1:PORT`, `http://localhost:PORT` (taken as 127.0.0.1, never resolved) or `http://[::1]:PORT`; no other scheme, host, userinfo, path or query, and port 0 is refused. A member or token that is not visible ASCII without spaces is refused too, so a value cannot split a request. Both refusals are `Error::Session`. - Reconnect: when the stream ends without a close, the reader reopens it (`reconnect_attempts`, default 5, `reconnect_delay` times n between tries), drops the new session's `hello`, and resubscribes each subscribed room from the last `seq` seen, so there is no gap and no repeat. When that fails the session is over and the client reports a session error. - Deadlines: opening a connection, sending a post and reading its status use `connect_timeout` (default 10 s). The stream read itself has none (heartbeats keep it busy; `close` shuts the socket, which unblocks it), as in the other libraries.

   **Why the binary stays network-free.** `TcpStream` is named in exactly one file, `src/client/http.rs`, declared `mod http;` directly under `#[cfg(feature = "client-http")]`. `client-http = ["client"]` is in no other feature, so `default`, `cli` and `client` never turn it on. `tests/no_telemetry.rs` allows `TcpStream` in that file only, while the parent declares it that way (no `TcpListener`, no resolver, no other socket type, anywhere), and new tests assert that no feature turns `client-http` on, that it brings in nothing but `client`, and that no other path gets the allowance. `klatch-server` asks for the feature in its dev-dependencies only.

   **Tests.** `proto/klatch-server/tests/client_http.rs` runs against the real `klatch-server http` in a throwaway home under /tmp, with a credential issued in process: the whole conformance scenario of `proto/klatch/tests/client_scenario` unchanged (the including crate now also provides `ATTRIBUTION`, `verified` here), a refused credential, urls refused before a spy listener sees a connection, header values refused, nothing listening, close, a reconnect with no gap and no repeat, and a killed server. The broken stream is injected with `HttpFault`, not provoked from the platform. A stand-in server checks the headers sent, `data:` lines as frames, a 429 retried and a 500 not retried. Every wait has a deadline and no stream is read to its end.

   **What this does not cover.** Windows and Linux runs (the suite ran on macOS); a read timeout on a half-open stream (the other libraries have none either).

278. **The whole client conformance scenario runs over HTTP against the real server (#1123; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** The Python and TypeScript suites run every step of the conformance scenario over stdio, over the daemon socket and now over `klatch-server http`, against a real server in a throwaway store, so the same events and sequence numbers are required of every transport (replay, live push, resume after a dropped stream, two members, direct messages). Every HTTP connection needs a worker credential, and only the human issues one for a real store, so the suites issue theirs with a test-only helper, the cargo example `issue_test_credential` of the `klatch-server` crate: `issue_test_credential MEMBER [CLEARANCE]` prints one token on stdout. Being an example it is not built by `cargo build`, not in a release archive, a wheel or the npm package, and `cargo test --workspace` builds it for the conformance step of CI. It refuses to run unless `KLATCH_HOME` is a directory under the system temp directory or /tmp (not the temp directory itself, with `..` resolved and symlinks followed) and `KLATCH_KEY_DIR` is inside it, so the live `~/.klatch` store and the Keychain are out of its reach by construction. The suites skip the HTTP run when the server or the helper is not built; a member's attribution over HTTP is `verified` (proven by the credential) where the other transports say `unattributed`.

279. **A klatch_wait over POST /mcp ends when the server stops or the client hangs up (#1125; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** **Question.** A klatch_wait over `POST /mcp` runs inside its connection handler for up to 55 s. How do SIGTERM and a departed client stop holding it?

   **Decision.** `McpServer::with_cancel` takes a check that the wait loop asks between its 100 ms polls; true ends the wait as a timeout with no messages (none were taken, so none are lost, and the client may call again). The `/mcp` handler passes a check of the daemon stop flag and of the client socket (a peek bounded to a millisecond, which reads end-of-file once the client has closed; nothing is consumed). So shutdown and the connection slot are released within about 100 ms of the event, not at the end of the wait.

   **Tests.** `tests/mcp_http.rs`: a client that leaves mid-wait frees its slot (cap 1) within 5 s; SIGTERM mid-wait stops the process within 5 s. Both were red before the change.

280. **The gate's leaked-sleep check counts only its own process group (#1101, #1127)** The check added in #1101 counted every `/bin/sleep 3600` on the machine, so a gate failed whenever another worktree, still on a base from before the watchdog fix, ran its own team tests at the same time. It now counts only sleeps in the gate's own process group, which an orphan keeps. The environment of a system binary such as `sleep` cannot be read on macOS, so a marker variable was not an option. A leak from a test that starts its own process group is covered by that test's own check (`test-team.sh`).

281. **`klatch mcp --stdio` and `klatch acp --stdio` judge a join by the verified harness (#1130; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** `klatch mcp --stdio` and `klatch acp --stdio` now build their session the way `serve --stdio` does (#1115): when the walk up the process tree verified a Claude Code session (`caller.claude_code`, #877), the session carries the Claude Code harness and a `klatch_join` is judged by it. The harness is never read from a request; a typed name, a credential, or no session above the process stays the generic floor. The ACP agent only answers looks today, so no join reaches it; it carries the same evidence so a later write method inherits the rule. This also meets #1003 for the stdio transports: a wire join carries no harness field and needs none, because the edge supplies the evidence it verified.

282. **A signed attestation of harness evidence from a remote host (#1138, design only; follows #1003; parked under the zero-trust epic #152 and remote exposure #1061, #157, #161; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** Decided by the human on 2026-11-21: a non-local peer (an ssh or HTTP member on another machine) may get harness-dependent room access (a `room-require` harness, #877) only with an attestation, never from a request field. Until one exists, a remote peer joins as the generic floor, as `serve --stdio --ssh-key` does today (#1115, #1130). This item is the design that the later build starts from. **It builds nothing and changes no code, schema or audit row.** It does not lift the block: every option below needs a real network transport (#157) and per-request authentication (#161) first, and a non-loopback bind is still refused (#1061).

   **Words.** The gateway host (G) holds the store and judges the join; it is the verifier. The remote host (R) runs the agent and the harness (Claude Code today) and reaches G over a transport that crosses a machine boundary. An attestation is a statement signed by a key G's human enrolled, saying what R's side verified about the process that is connecting. The member is never in it: who the peer is stays the job of the key or credential the transport already proves (#107: an ssh label mapped to a member by the human; #880: a key bundle). An attestation answers a different question, "what harness runs that member", and it is added to a peer that is already identified, never used to identify one.

   **1. Threat model.**

   What an attacker gains. (a) Entry to a verified-harness room (`room-require harness=claude-code`, and the capabilities that follow the harness, such as `pre-action-enforcement`): the room's other members then trust that the peer's posts passed a harness that enforces claims and hooks. (b) An advisory flag: a mark that says a member is on a verified harness, which a human or another agent may weigh. Neither is a clearance: a harness standing opens no sealed label, no label key and no admin floor action, and the design must keep it that way (see rule 7). The loss if the attestation is forged is therefore a wrong trust signal in a room, not a label breach. That bounds how much strength is worth paying for.

   Who attacks, and what each can fake today (before any attestation):

   | Attacker | Position | What they can forge | |---|---|---| | Network attacker | On the path between R and G | Anything, if the transport is plaintext or unauthenticated. Removed by #157 (mutual authentication, no plaintext off loopback, `policy::check_peer`); this design assumes that layer and adds nothing for it | | Hostile R host (its operator or root) | Owns the machine | Everything the host can sign or say: the process table, the registry, the signing key, the walk. An attestation from a hostile host is a true statement about nothing. No design that trusts the host can stop this; the answer is that the human enrolls only hosts it controls, and that revocation exists | | Hostile process of the same account on R | Same OS user as the honest agent | Forge the harness registry record (`$HOME/.claude/sessions`), start a process named `claude`, read a signing key that lives in the user's files, ask any same-user signer to sign. This is exactly the limit the local mechanism already documents for `runs_claude_code`: "same-user evidence ... keeps an honest session from being mislabelled, and does not stop a process of the same user that forges the registry" | | Hostile or prompt-injected agent inside a verified session on R | Inside the real harness | Cannot fake the harness (it really runs it) but can use a signer to attest for a different session, or run a non-harness process as a child and have it connect | | A different tenant on R | A second account on a shared R | Should fail: separate OS users make a key file and a socket private; this is where a signer with its own user matters | | Replayer | Captured an attestation once (a log, a backup, a compromised past session) | Reuse it on another connection, on another gateway, or later, or hand it to a different peer | | A host that was honest | R was compromised after enrollment | Keep attesting after the human stopped trusting it; revocation must reach a session that is already open (the #1083 problem) | | A local peer lying about being remote or the reverse | On G | Make a remote peer pass for local (to reach the process-tree path with a fake pid) or a local one for remote; the decision "remote" must come from the transport's own evidence kind, never an address or a field |

   What is fixed by construction in every option: the attested facts are only as good as the weakest of three links, (1) the verification on R (the walk and the registry), (2) the custody of the signing key on R, and (3) the binding of the signed statement to this one connection. An attestation raises a remote peer to **at most the strength of a local one, which is same-user evidence**, and never above it. It does not make a harness provable against a hostile host or a hostile same-user process. The product must say so in words (SECURITY.md, the room mark) and must not call a remote attested harness "verified" without a qualifier. The only evidence that proves a harness against its own user would come from the harness vendor (option 5 below) and does not exist.

   **2. What is attested.** A statement is a small canonical document, signed as a whole, versioned so a verifier refuses a version it does not know.

   | Field | Meaning | Why | |---|---|---| | `v` | statement version | refuse unknown, no downgrade | | `audience` | G's host id (a stable id G generated and shows `klatch doctor`) | a statement for one gateway is useless at another | | `nonce` | a value G issued for this connection and this join, single use | replay (rule 5) | | `channel` | a binding to the transport session (the TLS exporter value, or the ssh session id, whichever #157 gives) | a statement cannot move to another connection | | `harness` | the harness id the signer verified (`claude-code`) | the one thing a room reads | | `harness_version` | informational only, never judged | audit | | `session` | a keyed digest of the harness session id, never the raw id | stable continuity for the member's session, no id leaves R in the clear | | `tree` | a digest of the verified chain (program, pid, start time per hop) | audit and comparison between two attestations; **G cannot check it** (the pids are R's), so it never grants anything | | `not_before`, `not_after` | the validity window, short, set by G's policy as a ceiling | limits a stolen statement; the signer may choose shorter | | `signer` | the id of the enrolled key | which key to verify with, which to revoke |

   Not attested, on purpose: the member name (comes from the transport's key or credential), any clearance or label, any room, any capability other than the harness id. A statement that carried a member would turn it into a second identity path, and #881 shows why the typed name must stay out of the harness decision. A request field named `harness`, `attestation_of` or similar stays an error on every session (the `member-fixed` family of #107, extended when built); the statement travels in its own handshake step before the first request, never inside one.

   **3. Who signs.** Five options. Costs are stated as what is new, not as time.

   **Option 1. The credential declares the harness (no per-session statement).** The human records, when it enrols the ssh label or issues the key bundle, which harness that peer runs (`klatch admin ssh-key LABEL MEMBER --harness claude-code`, and the #880 shape for bundles: the launcher records the harness it started in the credential row). G reads it from its own table, never from the peer. No signature, no nonce, nothing on the wire, so no replay problem and no new crate. Strength: the same as the key it hangs on (whoever holds the key is that harness, by the human's word); it says nothing about whether the process using the key is a real harness today, and it cannot tell the same key on a laptop from the same key copied to a server. Cost: a policy value (no schema change for the ssh label; a column or a policy row for the bundle, which #880 already says is a design decision), one more floor action flag, tests. It is not an attestation in the ticket's sense (nothing is verified at join time); it is the cheapest honest version of "from the human, not from a field". Whether it satisfies the 2026-11-21 decision is question 1.

   **Option 2. The client binary on R signs with a host key (same user).** `klatch` on R, which already makes the walk for local commands, builds the statement and signs it with an attest key stored in the user's keystore or a 0600 file, then sends it in the handshake. Strength: honest-session mislabelling is stopped; a hostile same-user process reads the key or the registry and forges anything (the table row above). Cost: a signing crate through the dependency review (the weak-crypto refusal list applies; one Ed25519 implementation, no home-grown code), key generation and custody on R (#155), a handshake step and nonce store on G, enrolment, tests. No daemon on R.

   **Option 3. A klatch daemon on R signs, after the kernel names the caller.** R runs the daemon (#102), which already learns the caller's pid from the kernel (`SO_PEERCRED`, `getpeereid`, `LOCAL_PEERPID`) and already runs the walk and registry check on that pid (`peer_runs_claude_code`, #1096). It signs a statement for that pid's connection and no other. Strength: the client cannot ask for a statement about another session, because the pid comes from the kernel, not from the request (this closes the "agent attests for a different session" cell); with the daemon under its own OS user and the key private to it, it also closes the key-theft cell for other accounts, and it closes it for the same account only if the daemon runs as a different user (a design choice with a real deployment cost). It still trusts the registry, which the same account can write. Cost: the daemon must exist on R and be the way R's agents reach G (a proxy: the daemon's session to G carries the attestation of each local connection, so one daemon connection per local session, or a multiplexed channel); the signer code path; an enrolment of the daemon's key on G; and the `check_peer` extension. This is the closest to the existing local mechanism: the same verifier, running on R, with its result signed. It is the option that gives the ticket's "remote host's own gateway" meaning.

   **Option 4. The agent signs with a per-session key.** The harness or the agent generates a key at session start and signs. It proves only that one holder persists across the session (continuity), nothing about a harness, since the signer is the thing being judged. Use at most as a binding inside option 2 or 3 (a session key signed by the host key), never alone. Rejected as an attestation of harness.

   **Option 5. The harness vendor signs.** Claude Code (or Codex) signs its own session evidence with a vendor-held key chain. This is the only option that holds against a hostile user of R, and it does not exist; klatch cannot make it and must not pretend to. Recorded so that if a harness ever ships signed session evidence the verifier slot is already named: the statement version and the `signer` field leave room for a vendor chain. Not buildable now.

   **4. Key trust, enrolment and revocation.** Applies to options 2 and 3 (option 1 reuses the ssh-key and credential tables). - **Which key.** An Ed25519 attest key, separate from the transport key (a transport key proves who connects; an attest key proves what verified the connection; one compromised must not forge the other). Each attest key is bound to exactly one transport label, so a key enrolled for host A cannot be used on the session of host B (the same one-to-one rule as `ssh-key-mapped`). - **Who enrols.** The human, at a terminal, as a floor action (`FLOOR_ACTIONS`), so no agent and not the admin agent can add, change or remove one: `klatch admin attest-key LABEL <public key file> --harness claude-code`, stored in the policy table as `attest-key:<id>` (no schema change, as `ssh-key:<label>` is), audited as `attest-key` with the id and the label, never the key. The harness ids a key may attest are listed at enrolment, so a key enrolled for `claude-code` cannot attest `codex`. Key generation happens on R (the private half never travels); the human carries the public half to G, the way an `authorized_keys` line travels. - **How the human grants it.** The grant is the enrolment itself plus the room side: a room decides whether an attested remote harness counts (question 3), the same `room-require` switch, not a new one per key. - **Revocation.** `--off` removes the policy row. G rechecks it before each batch of requests and on each idle tick, as the ssh session does for its label (#1083, `Session::rechecked`), so an open session loses the harness standing at the next look. The standing is lost, not the session: the peer drops to the generic floor and rooms that require the harness refuse its next write; whether the session itself must also end is question 4. Every statement also expires (`not_after`), so a revoked key's statements die on their own even if the recheck were skipped. A refused statement (bad signature, unknown key, expired, wrong audience, reused nonce, harness not allowed for the key) is a kept refusal with a rule name (for example `attestation-invalid`), counted by the breaker with the other safety rules, so a host that keeps sending bad ones is stopped everywhere. - **Rotation.** More than one key per label, as ssh labels allow, so a key is replaced without a gap; the old one is removed with `--off`. - **Custody on R.** A 0600 file in a 0700 directory, or the OS keystore through the existing key code (#155, `klatch-keys`); a hardware-backed key (Secure Enclave, TPM) is the later hardening. A compromised key is a revoked key on G; there is nothing on R that G trusts without it.

   **5. Replay protection.** Four layers, all needed, none sufficient alone. (a) A **nonce from the verifier**: G issues a fresh random value at the start of each connection (one challenge frame, answered before the first request) and again before a `join` that needs the harness; the statement must sign it; G accepts each nonce once and keeps only the unexpired ones, in memory (no schema), so a restart forgets them and a statement is also bound by its window. (b) **Audience**: the statement names G's host id. (c) **Channel binding**: the statement names the transport session (TLS exporter or ssh session id), so a captured statement does not work on another connection even within its window and on the right gateway. (d) A **short window**: `not_after` is capped by G's policy and the clock check allows a stated skew; G never trusts R's clock beyond that cap. Over a transport with no round trip before the first request (none is planned: ssh and TLS both have one), a per-key monotonic counter replaces the nonce; not designed further. A nonce that the peer chose, or one carried inside a request, is refused.

   **6. How it fits the zero-trust maxim and the existing local mechanism.** - **On the wire.** The statement is accepted only on a transport `policy::check_peer` already passed (mutual TLS or ssh with a verified host key); a statement offered on a plaintext loopback listener, or on a stdio session of a local peer, is refused (a local peer has the process tree and needs no statement, and must not be able to substitute a forged one for the walk). The signature is authentication of data, not encryption: confidentiality is the transport's job, and the statement carries no message text and no raw session id. - **No trust from location.** "Remote" is decided by the transport's evidence kind (`SshForcedKey`, `Certificate`, `Bearer`), as `#1115` already did for ssh (this machine's process tree says nothing about a remote peer). An address or a request field never decides it. - **Per-request authentication (#161).** The harness standing is part of the principal the guard sees for each request, derived from verified evidence that has not expired, never cached past the window; credential expiry and rotation (#161) and attestation expiry are the same kind of fact and should share one recheck. - **The existing mechanism stays the model.** Locally, `runs_claude_code` is true only for `PeerEvidence::Session` found by the walk (live `claude` process with a matching registry pid and start time), and the harness reaches the judge through `Session::with_harness` (CLI commands, #877; `serve --stdio`, #1115; `mcp` and `acp`, #1130; the daemon per connection, #1096). A verified statement produces **the same value** for `with_harness`, so the `join` judge, `room-require` and the audit are unchanged; only the source of the value is new. A typed name still decides nothing about the harness (#881: the member comes from the key, the harness from the statement, so a typed name no longer has to cost the harness on a remote peer; locally it still does). A key bundle still proves a member and not a harness (#880): option 1 or a statement is what adds the harness to a bundle's session. Codex stays the floor (#884): an attester can only attest what it verified, and Codex writes no registry record, so a remote Codex needs the local Codex verification built first; the statement's `harness` field and the per-key allowed list make it per-harness. - **Show the difference.** The product marks where the standing came from (verified locally, attested by host LABEL, declared by credential), as `#990` marks an advisory sender, so a human reading a room can tell. The three are not the same strength.

   **7. Rules a build must keep.** 1. The harness is never read from a request field, a hello field, an environment variable or a header. 2. A statement never names a member and never changes who the peer is. 3. An attestation grants harness standing only: no clearance, no label key, no floor action, no grant (#893 is unchanged). 4. Fail closed: any doubt (unknown version, key, harness, bad signature, expired, audience or channel mismatch, reused nonce, an unreadable policy) is the generic floor, with a kept refusal. 5. Verified on a transport that passed `check_peer` only. 6. Revocation reaches an open session (recheck) and every statement expires. 7. Only the human enrols or removes an attest key (a floor action). 8. A new operation or evidence kind adds its rows to `proto/AUTHZ-AUDIT.md` and a test fails if it reaches the store without the guard (#161).

   **8. Tests a build starts from (red first).** A request field naming a harness is refused on every session; a forged statement (wrong key, flipped byte) leaves the floor and keeps a refusal; a replayed statement is refused (same nonce, other connection, other audience, after the window); a statement for a harness the key is not enrolled for is refused; `--off` ends the standing on an open session at the next look; a statement on plaintext loopback or on a local stdio session is refused; an attested peer still cannot open a sealed label or take a floor action; a typed name beside a statement changes nothing; a host that sends five bad statements is stopped by the breaker; two attest keys on two labels cannot be swapped.

   **9. Recommendation and order.** 1. **First, before anything here:** #157 (an encrypted, mutually authenticated transport with a verified host key, `check_peer` enforced), #161 (per-request authentication, credential expiry, and the audit that no operation skips the guard), and #155 (key custody) for the attest key's storage. Without a channel the statement could be bound to, nothing in rule 5 holds. #1061 stays as the checklist and its refusal test (a non-loopback bind refused) until then. 2. **Then option 1** (credential declares the harness), the smallest increment: reuses the ssh label and the key-bundle row (#880), needs no signing, rechecked by the #1083 mechanism, and gives remote members a harness standing the human chose, shown as "declared by credential". If the human decides that does not satisfy the 2026-11-21 decision, skip to step 3. 3. **Then option 3** (the daemon on R signs after the kernel names the caller and the walk verifies it) as the real attestation, with the nonce, audience, channel binding and window above, rechecked and revoked like step 2. It reuses the verifier that exists (#1096), so no new local logic is built, only the signer, the handshake step and the enrolment. 4. **Option 2** only where R has no daemon and the human accepts same-user strength, labelled as such. **Options 4 and 5** are not built; option 5 is watched for. 5. Codex and other harnesses wait for their own local verification (#884, #64) and are then added as allowed ids, not as a new mechanism.

   **10. Open questions for the human.** 1. Does option 1 (the human declares the harness of a credential, nothing verified per join) satisfy the 2026-11-21 decision, or must the standing come from a verified per-session signature (option 3) before any remote peer gets it? 2. Is same-user strength acceptable for a remote harness standing (a hostile process of the same account on R can forge it), given that it opens no clearance? Or must remote standing wait for a signer under a different OS user on R, or for vendor-signed evidence? 3. Should a room be able to say which standing it accepts (verified locally only, or also attested, or also declared by credential), or does `harness=claude-code` mean any of them? The first costs one more room setting and keeps the strongest rooms local-only. 4. When a key is revoked or a statement expires on an open session, should the session drop to the generic floor and stay open (this design), or end, as an unmapped ssh label does (#1083)? 5. Who may enrol the attest key: only the human at a terminal on G (this design), or may a remote enrolment be proposed from R and approved by the human on G, as a pairing step? 6. Is a daemon on every remote host acceptable (option 3), or must a remote agent work with only the `klatch` binary and an ssh key (options 1 and 2)? 7. Should the statement carry the process-tree digest at all, given G cannot check it and it grants nothing? It is kept here for audit only; it can be dropped to keep the statement small and the private facts of R's process table off the wire. 8. The attest key needs custody on R (#155): file only at first, or wait for the OS keystore and a hardware-backed key before any remote use? 9. Does the lifetime ceiling of a statement belong in G's policy table (this design) or fixed in code?

283. **Daemon test harness tolerates a socket the peer closed at once (#1150; test only; no schema change; 2026-11-21).** `the_connection_cap_refuses_the_excess_with_a_frame_and_frees_a_slot_when_one_closes` failed once on macOS with "Invalid argument". The daemon closes a refused connection straight after the refusal frame, and on macOS a client that sets a socket option (`set_read_timeout`) after the peer has closed gets EINVAL. `Conn::open` in `klatch-server/tests/daemon.rs` unwrapped that call. It now ignores an `InvalidInput` error there only (the read that follows returns the refusal frame or end of file at once, so the timeout is not needed); any other error still panics. The server path is unchanged: a real client reading a refusal never sets an option after connecting. A new test, `a_connection_the_peer_closed_at_once_still_opens_and_reads_the_end`, reproduces the shape (accept then close before the client sets an option). It cannot be red on Linux, where the option call succeeds; only a macOS run exercises the fix. The other tickets in the flaky-test triage (#1081, #1142, #752, #829, #697) were already fixed or are not reproducible; the evidence is in the ticket comments.
